is indeed the true equilibrium measure for
V_{n}(x)
by verifying that the Euler-Lagrange variational conditions for the energy minimization problem are satisfied (for large enough
n
). We will furthermore show that
\psi_{n}(x)
is ‘regular’ in the sense that it is positive in
(-1,1)
and vanishes like a square root as
|x|\to 1
. This means we will get Airy universality near the endpoints
x=\pm 1
.

S2.4.2
Support of the equilibrium measure

Lemma S6
.

Suppose
Q\in\mathrm{VSLF}(p,q)
with
p\geq 1
, and
q
arbitrary for
p>1
but constrained to
q>-1
for
p=1
.
Then there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
M>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have
h_{n}(x)>C
for all
|x|<M
.

We defer the proof of this statement to
Section

S5.2
. The fact that for sufficiently large
n
we have
h_{n}(x)>0
for
|x|\leq 1
implies via
Eq.

S2.14
that the equilibrium measure
\psi_{n}(x)>0
for
|x|<1
and vanishes like a square root as
|x|\to 1
, as claimed.

S2.4.3
Check of the Euler-Lagrange variational conditions

Lemma S7
.

Define
g_{n}(z)\coloneqq\int_{-1}^{1}\log(z-t)\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t
for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
. Then for large enough
n
the following Euler-Lagrange variational conditions are satisfied (c.f.
[
62
]
).

(i)

g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n}=0
, for
|x|\leq 1
.

(ii)

g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n}<0
, for
|x|>1
.

The strict inequality in
(ii)
means that
\psi_{n}
is ‘regular’ (for large enough
n
).

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the discussion in
[
116
, Theorem IV.3.1]
, and does not require a large
n
limit. Part (ii) follows by combining
Lemma

S6
with the relation

g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n}=-\int_{1}^{x}h_{n}(t)\sqrt{t^{2}-1}\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\text{for }|x|>1,

(S2.15)

which we will now prove. We essentially rehash the relevant part of the proof of
[
64
, Lemma 3.2]
. We start from the Hilbert transform

H\psi_{n}(t)\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{\pi}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\psi_{n}(s)}{t-s}\mathrm{d}s,

(S2.16)

where
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int
denotes a principal value integral, and integrate
t
from 1 to
x
to give

\int_{1}^{x}H\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\dfrac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{n}(s)\left(\log{|x-s|}-\log{|1-s|}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

(S2.17)

(This integration is justified for sufficiently smooth
\psi_{n}
; see proof of
[
116
, Theorem IV.3.1]
.) Since
g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)=2\int_{-1}^{1}\log{|x-s|}\psi_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, this means

g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-\left[g_{n,+}(1)+g_{n,-}(1)\right]=2\pi\int_{1}^{x}H\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t.

(S2.18)

The variational condition gives
g_{n,+}(1)+g_{n,-}(1)=V_{n}(1)+l_{n}
, and so

g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n}=2\pi\int_{1}^{x}\left(H\psi_{n}(t)-\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(t)}{2\pi}\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(S2.19)

It remains to show that

H\psi_{n}(t)-\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(t)}{2\pi}=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi}h_{n}(t)\sqrt{t^{2}-1},\quad\text{for }t>1.

(S2.20)

This will follow the proof of
[
64
, Theorem 3.1]
. Define the function

F_{n}(z)\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{\pi i}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{\psi_{n}(s)}{s-z}\mathrm{d}s,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1],

(S2.21)

which on
[-1,1]
has the jump

F_{n,\pm}(x)=\pm\psi_{n}(x)+iH\psi_{n}(x),\quad x\in[-1,1].

(S2.22)

However, differentiating the variational condition on
[-1,1]
gives

H\psi_{n}(x)=\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(x)}{2\pi},\quad x\in[-1,1].

(S2.23)

It follows that
F_{n}
satisfies the following scalar Riemann-Hilbert problem,

\displaystyle F_{n,+}(x)+F_{n,-}(x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{iV_{n}^{\prime}(x)}{\pi},\quad x\in[-1,1],

(S2.24)

\displaystyle F_{n,+}(x)-F_{n,-}(x)

\displaystyle=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1],

(S2.25)

\displaystyle F_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{-1}{i\pi z}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-2}),\quad z\to\infty,

(S2.26)

which has the standard solution

F_{n}(z)=r(z)\dfrac{1}{\pi i}\int_{-1}^{1}\dfrac{iV_{n}^{\prime}(s)/(2\pi)}{r_{+}(s)}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{s-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

(S2.27)

where
r(z)=(z-1)^{1/2}(z+1)^{1/2}
. For nonreal
z
in the domain of analyticity of
V_{n}
, we can rewrite

F_{n}(z)=\dfrac{iV_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{2\pi}+\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}h_{n}(z)r(z),

(S2.28)

where
h_{n}(z)
is given by the contour integral

h_{n}(z)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma_{z}}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{r(s)}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{s-z},

(S2.29)

which for
z\to x\in[-1,1]
gives the familiar expression for
h_{n}(x)
from the definition of the equilibrium measure. For
t>1
we have

F_{n}(t)=\dfrac{iV_{n}^{\prime}(t)}{2\pi}+\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}h_{n}(t)\sqrt{t^{2}-1},

(S2.30)

and the expression
Eq.

S2.20
comes from noticing that
H\psi_{n}(t)=-iF_{n}(t)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
.
∎

S2.5
Logarithmic transform of the equilibrium measure, and the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
T

Given the rescaled equilibrium measure
\psi_{n}(x)
for
V_{n}(x)=Q(\beta_{n}x)/n
(see
Section

S2.4
), we define its logarithmic transform
g_{n}(z)
by

g_{n}(z)\coloneqq\int_{-1}^{1}\log(z-t)\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t,\quad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,1],

(S2.31)

where we take the principal branch of the logarithm. For
z
approaching the real axis, we have the limiting values
g_{n,\pm}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{y\to 0^{+}}g_{n}(x\pm iy)
given by

g_{n,\pm}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\log{|x-t|}\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t\pm\begin{cases}i\pi,&x\leq 1,\\
i\pi\int_{x}^{1}\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t,&-1<x<1,\\
0,&x\geq 1.\end{cases}

(S2.32)

The following proposition is immediate from
Eqs.

S2.31
and
S2.32
.

Proposition S1
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following holds.

(a)

g_{n}
is analytic and
g_{n}|_{\mathbb{C}_{\pm}}
have continuous extensions to
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
.

(b)

The map
z\mapsto e^{ng_{n}(z)}
possesses an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
, and

e^{ng_{n}(z)}z^{-n}=1+\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{|z|}\right),\quad\text{as }z\to\infty.

(S2.33)

In terms of the logarithmic transform
g_{n}(z)
, we define the matrix-valued function
T
by

T(z)=e^{-\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}U(z)e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}e^{-ng_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}.

(S2.34)

Given that
U(z)
satisfies the Riemann-Hilbert problem
(U_{a})
-
(U_{d})
, one can combine
Eqs.

S2.32
and
S2.33
to verify that
T(z)
satisfies the following equivalent Riemann-Hilbert problem.

(T_{a})

T(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
.

(T_{b})

T
satisfies the following jump relations on
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle T_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=T_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}e^{-n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}&|x|^{\rho}\\
0&e^{n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for }x\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\},

(S2.35)

\displaystyle T_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=T_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}1&|x|^{\rho}e^{n(g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n})}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for }|x|>1.

(S2.36)

(T_{c})

T(z)=\mathds{1}+\mathcal{O}(1/z)
as
z\to\infty
.

(T_{d})

T(z)
has the same behavior as
Y(z)
as
z\to 0
, given by
Eq.

S2.3
.

Notice that, by
(T_{c})
, the Riemann-Hilbert problem is now normalized at infinity. Furthermore, by
Lemmas

S6
and
S7
, the jump matrix is exponentially close to the identity for
|x|>1
. For
|x|<1
, we see from
Eq.

S2.32
that the diagonal elements of the jump matrix are complex phases which rapidly oscillate for large
n
; these can be viewed as the analogue of the ‘fast phase’ arising in the analysis of linear differential equations in the WKB limit
[
62
]
.

S2.6
Analytic continuation of the equilibrium measure and its logarithmic transform

In terms of
h_{n}(z)
, which is defined by
Eq.

S2.9
within the domain of analyticity of
V_{n}(z)
, we can define an analytic continuation
\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)
of the equilibrium measure by

\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}r(z)h_{n}(z),

(S2.37)

with
r(z)=(z+1)^{1/2}(z-1)^{1/2}
. Since
h_{n}(z)
is analytic, while
r(z)
flips sign across
[-1,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{n,+}(x)=-\hat{\psi}_{n,-}(x)=\psi_{n}(x),\quad\text{for }x\in(-1,1),

(S2.38)

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{n,+}(x)=\hat{\psi}_{n,-}(x),\quad\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1),

(S2.39)

so
\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)
is an analytic continuation of
\psi_{n}(x)
to
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and similarly
-\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)
provides an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. Finally, having defined these continuations, we define

\phi_{n}(z)\coloneqq-\pi i\int_{1}^{z}\hat{\psi}_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

(S2.40)

where the path of integration does not cross the real axis. By using the analyticity of
\hat{\psi}_{n}(z)
and the fact that
\int_{0}^{1}\psi_{n}(x)\mathrm{d}x=1/2
by symmetry, one can show that
\phi_{n}(z)
can equivalently be written as

\phi_{n}(z)=\pm\dfrac{i\pi}{2}-\pi i\int_{0}^{z}\hat{\psi}_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm},

(S2.41)

where again the path of integration does not cross the real axis. Note that
Eq.

S2.39
implies that
\phi_{n}(z)
is analytic across
\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)
. Also,
Eq.

S2.41
shows that
2\phi_{n,+}(0)=2\pi i\int_{0}^{1}\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\pi i
. This is a special property of
even
weight functions, but should be true generically given that symmetry. This factor (exponentiated) is what ends up giving rise to the
(-1)^{n}
staggering factor in the recurrence coefficients, so here we see that this is a direct consequence of the
x\to-x
symmetry, which itself is a consequence of unitarity.

For the logarithmic transform, note that by
Eq.

S2.32
we have

g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x)=\begin{cases}2\pi i,&x\leq-1,\\
2\pi i\int_{x}^{1}\psi_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t,&|x|<1,\\
0,&x\geq 1.\end{cases}

(S2.42)

From
Eqs.

S2.38
and
S2.40
we deduce that the functions
2\phi_{n}(z)
and
-2\phi_{n}(z)
provide analytic continuations of
(g_{n,+}-g_{n,-})(z)
into the upper and lower half planes respectively.

S2.7
Riemann-Hilbert problem for
S
: contour deformation and ‘opening the lens’

Next we notice that the jump matrix for
T(x)
on
x\in[-1,1]
can be factorized as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}e^{-n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}&|x|^{\rho}\\
0&e^{n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

(S2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
|x|^{-\rho}e^{n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&|x|^{\rho}\\
-|x|^{-\rho}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
|x|^{-\rho}e^{-n(g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x))}&1\end{pmatrix}.

Remembering that
\pm 2\phi_{n}(z)
provides an analytic continuation of
g_{n,+}-g_{n,-}
to
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
, and defining the analytic continuation of
|x|^{\rho}
to be

\omega(z)=\begin{cases}(-z)^{\rho},\quad\text{if }\operatorname{Re}{z}<0,\\
z^{\rho},\quad\text{if }\operatorname{Re}{z}>0,\end{cases}

(S2.44)

with principal branches of powers, we define the matrix-valued function
S
as follows.

S(z)=\begin{cases}T(z),&\text{for }z\text{ outside the lens},\\
T(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\omega(z)^{-1}e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{for }z\text{ in the upper parts of the lens},\\
T(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\omega(z)^{-1}e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{for }z\text{ in the lower parts of the lens}.\end{cases}

(S2.45)

By the upper parts of the lens, we mean the regions enclosed by
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{4}\cup\Sigma_{5}
, and by the lower parts of the lens, we mean the regions enclosed by
\Sigma_{3}\cup\Sigma_{2}
and
\Sigma_{6}\cup\Sigma_{5}
, with the contours
\Sigma_{j}
shown schematically in
Fig.

S1
. Because of the shape of these contours, this step is often referred to as ‘opening the lens’.

\Sigma_{5}
\Sigma_{2}
\Sigma_{7}
\Sigma_{8}
\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{3}
\Sigma_{4}
\Sigma_{6}
-1
1
0

Figure S1:
The lens shaped contour
\Sigma=\bigcup_{j=1}^{6}\Sigma_{j}
.

Given the conditions for the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
T
, one can verify that
S
is the unique solution of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

(S_{a})

S(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
.

(S_{b})

S
satisfies the following jump relations on
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle S_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=S_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\omega(z)^{-1}e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for }z\in\Sigma\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm},

(S2.46)

\displaystyle S_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=S_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}0&|x|^{\rho}\\
-|x|^{-\rho}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for }x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\},

(S2.47)

\displaystyle S_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=S_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}1&|x|^{\rho}e^{n(g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-V_{n}(x)-l_{n})}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{for }|x|>1.

(S2.48)

(S_{c})

S(z)=\mathds{1}+\mathcal{O}(1/z)
as
z\to\infty
.

(S_{d})

For
\rho<0
, the matrix function
S(z)
has the following behavior as
z\to 0
:

S(z)=\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&|z|^{\rho}\\
1&|z|^{\rho}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{as }z\to 0,z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma.

(S2.49)

For
\rho>0
, the matrix function
S(z)
has the following behavior as
z\to 0
:

S(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{as }z\to 0\text{ from outside the lens},\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}|z|^{-\rho}&1\\
|z|^{-\rho}&1\end{pmatrix},&\text{as }z\to 0\text{ from inside the lens}.\end{cases}

(S2.50)

We already know from
Lemma

S7
that the jump matrix for
|x|>1
is exponentially close in
n
to the identity matrix. For the new jump on
\Sigma\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
, we will show in
Eq.

S5.63
that
\sup_{z\in\Sigma}|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, so this jump matrix will also tend to the identity as
n\to\infty
. Precisely how fast this happens depends on the scaling of
\operatorname{Re}[\phi_{n}(z)]
with
n
. We will see later that in most cases we can get
\exp[-n\phi_{n}(z)]
to decay superpolynomially in
n
. However, in the marginal case where the weight
w(x)
decays only exponentially as
|x|\to\infty
, the decay of
\exp[-n\phi_{n}(z)]
with
n
will be slower, only super-polylogarithmic in the worst case (see
Eq.

S5.63
for details).

S2.8
Parametrix
N
for the outside region

Since the jumps for
S(z)
for
z
away from
(-1,1)
decay with
n
, it is useful to solve the Riemann-Hilbert problem for only the jump on
(-1,1)
, whose solution we will denote by
N(z)
.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
N

(N_{a})

N:\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(N_{b})

N_{+}(x)=N_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}0&|x|^{\rho}\\
-|x|^{-\rho}&0\end{pmatrix}
for
x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}
.

(N_{c})

N(z)=\mathds{1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{|z|}\right)
as
|z|\to\infty
.

We will construct a solution to this RH problem in terms of the Szegő function
D
associated with
|x|^{\rho}
on
(-1,1)
. This is a scalar function which is the solution to a multiplicative scalar RH problem with the following conditions.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
D

(D_{a})

D:\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]\to\mathbb{C}
is analytic and nonzero.

(D_{b})

D_{+}(x)D_{-}(x)=|x|^{\rho}
for
x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}
.

(D_{c})

\lim_{z\to\infty}D(z)=D_{\infty}
exists and is a positive real number.

In this case, one can check that the Szegő function is given by

D(z)=\dfrac{z^{\rho/2}}{\varphi(z)^{\rho/2}},

(S2.51)

where

\varphi(z)\coloneqq z+(z+1)^{1/2}(z-1)^{1/2}

(S2.52)

is the conformal map from
\mathbb{C}\setminus[-1,1]
to the exterior of the unit circle;
\varphi(z)
has a branch cut along
[-1,1]
and behaves like
\varphi(z)\approx 2z
as
z\to\infty
.

Having obtained
D
, it turns out that the solution to the RH problem for
N
is
[
66
]

N(z)=\begin{pmatrix}D_{\infty}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{D_{\infty}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{a(z)+a^{-1}(z)}{2}&\frac{a(z)-a^{-1}(z)}{2i}\\
\frac{a(z)-a^{-1}(z)}{-2i}&\frac{a(z)+a^{-1}(z)}{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{D(z)}&0\\
0&D(z)\end{pmatrix},

(S2.53)

where

a(z)\coloneqq\dfrac{(z-1)^{1/4}}{(z+1)^{1/4}}\quad\text{and}\quad D_{\infty}\coloneqq\lim_{z\to\infty}D(z)=2^{-\rho/2}.

(S2.54)

Since
N(z)
is a product of three matrices each with
\det=1
, we have
\det{N(z)}=1
, so
N(z)
is invertible. Furthermore, as
z\to\infty
, this matrix scales as

N(z)=\mathds{1}+\dfrac{i}{2z}\begin{pmatrix}0&2^{-\rho}\\
-2^{\rho}&0\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{z^{2}}\right),

(S2.55)

and as
z\to 0
it scales as

N(z)\sim\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}z^{-\rho/2}&z^{\rho/2}\\
z^{-\rho/2}&z^{\rho/2}\end{pmatrix},

(S2.56)

where the big-
\mathcal{O}
notation is taken elementwise.

S2.9
Local parametrix
P
near the endpoints
z=\pm 1

While it is true that the jumps of
S(z)
decay with
n
for
z
away from the real axis, these jumps become nonnegligible at the points
z=0
and
z=\pm 1
where the contour
\Sigma
meet the real axis. We need to construct ‘local parametrices’ which approximately solve the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
S
near these points, and handle any potential singularities.

In this subsection we will construct the parametrices for the endpoints
z=\pm 1
, which we will build out of Airy functions (because the equilibrium measure is regular for large enough
n
). Our analysis here is standard and will closely follow that of Refs.
[
62
,
49
]
. First we focus on the parametrix near
z=1
, since the construction near
z=-1
will be closely related by symmetry. We will make use of the function
h_{n}(z)
defined in
Eq.

S2.9
, which is analytic in the region of analyticity of
V_{n}
, which by assumption contains a neighbourhood of
z=1
. We will construct the parametrix
P(z)
in a disk
U_{\delta_{2}}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z-1|<\delta_{2}\}
around
z=1
, where the radius
\delta_{2}>0
is a small constant.
P(z)
is the solution to the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
P

(P_{a})

P
is analytic in
U_{\delta_{2}}\setminus\Sigma
.

(P_{b})

P_{+}(z)=P_{-}(z)v_{S}(z)
for
z\in\Sigma\cap U_{\delta_{2}}
, with
v_{S}
the jump matrix for
S
.

(P_{c})

P(z)N(z)^{-1}\sim\mathds{1}+\mathcal{O}(1/n)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
z
on the boundary
\partial U_{\delta}
of the disk
U_{\delta}
for
\delta
in compact subsets of
(0,\delta_{2})
.

The construction of
P
involves three steps. First we will construct a matrix-valued function that satisfies conditions
(P_{a})
and
(P_{b})
. In order to do this we will transform this RHP into a RHP for
P^{(1)}
with constant jump matrices and then construct a solution of the latter RHP. Finally, we will take the matching condition
(P_{c})
into account.

\gamma_{\sigma,2}
\gamma_{\sigma,4}
\gamma_{\sigma,1}
\gamma_{\sigma,3}
I
IV
II
III
\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
0
\sigma
\sigma

Figure S2:
The oriented contour
\gamma_{\sigma}
and the jump matrix
v_{1}
for
\Psi
on
\gamma_{\sigma}
. The four straight rays
\gamma_{\sigma,1},\dots,\gamma_{\sigma,4}
divide the complex plane into four regions I, II, III, and IV.

S2.9.1
Transformation to constant jump matrices

Following Ref.
[
49
]
, in order to transform to constant jump matrices, we seek the parametrix
P
near
1
in the following form

P(z)=E(z)P^{(1)}(z)e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}z^{-(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}},\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta_{2}}\setminus\Sigma,

(S2.57)

with
E
an invertible analytic matrix-valued function in
U_{\delta_{2}}
, to be determined to ensure that the matching condition
(P_{c})
is satisfied. Since
E
is invertible and analytic, one can use
Eqs.

S2.38
,
S2.39
and
S2.40
to show that the jump matrix for
P^{(1)}
is piecewise constant:

P^{(1)}_{+}(z)=\begin{cases}P^{(1)}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},&z\in(\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{3})\cap U_{\delta_{2}},\\
P^{(1)}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},&z\in\Sigma_{2}\cap U_{\delta_{2}}=(1-\delta_{2},1),\\
P^{(1)}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&z\in\Sigma_{8}\cap U_{\delta_{2}}=(1,1+\delta_{2}).\end{cases}

(S2.58)

We will determine
P^{(1)}
subject to these constant jump matrices by an explicit construction based on an auxiliary problem for
\Psi(\zeta)
in the
\zeta
-plane with jumps on the oriented contour
\gamma_{\sigma}=\cup_{j=1}^{4}\gamma_{\sigma,j}
, shown in
Fig.

S2
, consisting of four straight rays

\gamma_{\sigma,1}:\arg{\zeta}=\sigma,\quad\gamma_{\sigma,2}:\arg{\zeta}=\pi,\quad\gamma_{\sigma,3}:\arg{\zeta}=-\sigma,\quad\gamma_{\sigma,4}:\arg{\zeta}=0,

with
\sigma\in(\pi/3,\pi)
. These four rays divide the complex plane into four regions I, II, III, and IV, also shown in
Fig.

S2
.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\Psi

(\Psi_{a})

\Psi
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\gamma_{\sigma}
.

(\Psi_{b})

\Psi_{+}(\zeta)=\Psi_{-}(\zeta)v_{1}(\zeta)
for
\zeta\in\gamma_{\sigma}
, where
v_{1}
is the piecewise constant matrix-valued function on
\gamma_{\sigma}
defined as shown in
Fig.

S2
, i.e.,
v_{1}(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}
for
\zeta\in\gamma_{\sigma,1}
, etc. This means that
\Psi
has the same jumps on
\gamma_{\sigma}
as
P^{(1)}
does on
\Sigma_{S}\cap U_{\delta_{2}}
.

(\Psi_{c})

\Psi
has the following behaviour at infinity,

\displaystyle\Psi(\zeta)\sim\,

\displaystyle\zeta^{-\sigma_{3}/4}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}

(S2.59)

\displaystyle\times\left[\mathds{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}\right)^{-k}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{k}(s_{k}+t_{k})&s_{k}-t_{k}\\
(-1)^{k}(s_{k}-t_{k})&s_{k}+t_{k}\end{pmatrix}\right]e^{-(\pi i/4)\sigma_{3}}e^{-(2/3)\zeta^{3/2}\sigma_{3}},

as
\zeta\to\infty
, uniformly for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\gamma_{\sigma}
and
\sigma
in compact subsets of
(\pi/3,\pi)
. Here,

s_{k}=\dfrac{\Gamma\left(3k+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{54^{k}k!\Gamma\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)},\quad t_{k}=-\dfrac{6k+1}{6k-1}s_{k},\quad\text{for }k\geq 1.

(S2.60)

It is well known
[
65
,
62
]
that, with
\varpi\coloneqq e^{2\pi i/3}
and
\Ai
the Airy function, the solution to this RHP is

\Psi(\zeta)=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{-\pi i/12}\times\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\zeta)&\Ai(\varpi^{2}\zeta)\\
\Ai^{\prime}(\zeta)&\varpi^{2}\Ai^{\prime}(\varpi^{2}\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-(\pi i/6)\sigma_{3}},&\zeta\in\text{I},\\
\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\zeta)&\Ai(\varpi^{2}\zeta)\\
\Ai^{\prime}(\zeta)&\varpi^{2}\Ai^{\prime}(\varpi^{2}\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-(\pi i/6)\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix},&\zeta\in\text{II},\\
\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\zeta)&-\varpi^{2}\Ai(\varpi\zeta)\\
\Ai^{\prime}(\zeta)&-\Ai^{\prime}(\varpi\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-(\pi i/6)\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},&\zeta\in\text{III},\\
\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\zeta)&-\varpi^{2}\Ai(\varpi\zeta)\\
\Ai^{\prime}(\zeta)&-\Ai^{\prime}(\varpi\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-(\pi i/6)\sigma_{3}},&\zeta\in\text{IV},\\
\end{cases}

(S2.61)

With this definition, we can now specify the precise form of the contour
\Sigma
near
z=1
:
\Sigma
is defined in
U_{\delta_{2}}
as the inverse
f_{n}
-image of
\gamma_{\sigma}\cap f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}})
, where
f_{n}(z)
is a biholomorphic map between the
z
- and
\zeta
-planes to be constructed in the next section. We then define

P^{(1)}(z)\coloneqq\Psi(f_{n}(z)),\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta_{2}}\setminus f_{n}^{-1}(\gamma_{\sigma}).

(S2.62)

S2.9.2
Construction of biholomorphic map

Proposition S2
.

There exists a
\delta_{2}>0
such that for sufficiently large
n
there exists a function
f_{n}:U_{\delta_{2}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}})\subset\mathbb{C}
with the following properties.

(a)

f_{n}:U_{\delta_{2}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}})
is biholomorphic.

(b)

f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}}\cap\mathbb{R})=f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}})\cap\mathbb{R}
,
f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm})=f_{n}(U_{\delta_{2}})\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
.

(c)

\frac{2}{3}(f_{n}(z))^{3/2}=-n\phi_{n}(z)
for all
z\in U_{\delta_{2}}\setminus(-\infty,1]
.

Proof (adapted from
[
49
]
, Prop 3.19).

Given the analyticity of
h_{n}(z)
near
z=1
, we will explicitly construct
f_{n}(z)
as follows.
We define the auxiliary function
\hat{\varphi}_{n}:\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,1]\to\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{3}{4}(z-1)^{-3/2}\int_{1}^{z}h_{n}(s)(s+1)^{1/2}(s-1)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}s,

(S2.63)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{h_{n}(1)}{\sqrt{2}}+\dfrac{3}{4}(z-1)^{-3/2}\int_{1}^{z}\left[h_{n}(s)(s+1)^{1/2}-h_{n}(1)\sqrt{2}\right](s-1)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}s.

(S2.64)

Note that
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
actually has no jumps across
(-1,1)
because both
(z-1)^{-3/2}
and
(s-1)^{1/2}
flip sign, so we already know that
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
has an analytic continuation to
U_{\delta}\setminus\{1\}
for some
\delta>0
. To deal with the potential difficulty at
z=1
, note that, for
|s-1|<\delta
, Cauchy’s theorem tells us that

\displaystyle\left|h_{n}(s)(s+1)^{1/2}-h_{n}(1)\sqrt{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(s-1)\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|w-1|=2\delta}\dfrac{h_{n}(w)(w+1)^{1/2}-h_{n}(1)\sqrt{2}}{(w-1)(w-s)}\mathrm{d}w\right|,

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{|s-1|}{\delta}\sup_{|w-1|=2\delta}\left|h_{n}(w)(w+1)^{1/2}-h_{n}(1)\sqrt{2}\right|.

In
Eq.

S5.56
we prove that, for sufficiently large
n
and sufficiently small
\delta
, there exists a constant
\epsilon>0
(independent of
n
) such that
|h_{n}(w)-h_{n}(1)|<\epsilon
for
|w-1|<2\delta
. Together with
Eq.

57
, which tells us that
h_{n}(1)=2p+o(1)
, it is easy to see that there is a constant
c>0
such that for
|s-1|<\delta
and sufficiently large
n
we have

\left|h_{n}(s)(s+1)^{1/2}-h_{n}(1)\sqrt{2}\right|\leq c|s-1|.

(S2.65)

Inserting this into
Eq.

S2.64
, we conclude there is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that for sufficiently large
n
we have

|\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)-\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)|\leq C_{1}|z-1|,\quad\text{for }|z-1|<\delta,

(S2.66)

where
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)=h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2}
. Therefore the isolated singularity of
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
at
z=1
is removable, so that
\hat{\varphi}_{n}
is indeed analytic for
z\in U_{\delta}
. Having established these properties of
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
, we then define the function

\varphi_{n}(z)\coloneqq(z-1)\left(\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)\right)^{2/3},\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta}.

(S2.67)

Since
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)=h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2}
and
h_{n}(1)=2p+o(1)
by
Eq.

57
, we conclude that
\operatorname{Re}[\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)]>0
for
z\in U_{\delta}
and sufficiently large
n
. This implies that
\varphi_{n}(z)
is analytic for
z\in U_{\delta}
.

To establish that
\varphi_{n}(z)
is injective, observe that, by
Eqs.

S2.66
,
S2.67
,
57
and
S5.56
,
\varphi_{n}(z)
is uniformly (in
n
and
z
) bounded in
U_{\delta}
. By Cauchy’s theorem for derivatives, this implies that
\varphi_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)
is also uniformly (in
n
and
z
) bounded in
U_{\delta}
for a smaller
\delta
. Since
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)=h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2}\neq 0
, we have
\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(1)=(h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2})^{2/3}
, so that

\left|\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(z)-(h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2})^{2/3}\right|=\left|\int_{1}^{z}\varphi_{n}^{\prime\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right|\leq C_{2}|z-1|,\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta},

for some constant
C_{2}>0
and sufficiently large
n
. By
Eq.

57
we know there exists
h_{0}>0
such that
h_{n}(1)>h_{0}
for sufficiently large
n
, and so by the inverse function theorem we conclude there exists
0<\delta_{2}<\delta
such that
\varphi_{n}
is injective and hence biholomorphic in
U_{\delta_{2}}
.

Finally, we define the biholomorphic map
f_{n}(z)
by

f_{n}(z)\coloneqq n^{2/3}\varphi_{n}(z).

(S2.68)

Property (b) follows from the fact that
\varphi_{n}
and its inverse
\varphi_{n}^{-1}
are real on the real axis, while property (c) follows from

\phi_{n}(z)=-\dfrac{2}{3}(z-1)^{3/2}\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z),

(S2.69)

which follows from
Eqs.

S2.40
and
S2.63
.

∎

To calculate residues we will need the values of
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)
and
\hat{\varphi}_{n}^{\prime}(1)
, which can be calculated from the values of
h_{n}(1)
and
h_{n}^{\prime}(1)
via

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}h_{n}(1),

(S2.70)

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{40}\left[h_{n}(1)+4h_{n}^{\prime}(1)\right].

(S2.71)

S2.9.3
Satisfying the matching condition

In this final step, we construct the invertible analytic matrix-valued function
E(z)
to ensure that the matching function
(P_{c})
is satisfied. In the
n\to\infty
limit, by comparing
Eqs.

S2.62
,
S2.59
and
S2.68
, we see that we must define
E
as

E(z)\coloneqq N(z)z^{(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}}e^{(\pi i/4)\sigma_{3}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}f_{n}(z)^{\sigma_{3}/4},\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta_{2}}.

(S2.72)

It is straightforward to check that
E(z)
so defined is indeed analytic and invertible (see
[
49
]
, Remark 3.21). This ends the construction of the parametrix
P
near
z=1
.

S2.9.4
Summary of the local analysis near
z=1

Fixing an angle
\sigma\in(\pi/3,\pi)
, we have defined the contour
\Sigma
in a disk
U_{\delta_{2}}
near
z=1
as the inverse image of the contour
\gamma_{\sigma}
under the map
f_{n}
defined in
Eq.

S2.68
. We have then found a solution

P(z)=E(z)\Psi(f_{n}(z))e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}z^{-(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}},\quad\text{for }z\in\Sigma\cap U_{\delta_{2}}

(S2.73)

to the RHP for
P
, where the matrix-valued function
E
is defined in
Eq.

S2.72
, and the matrix-valued function
\Psi
is defined in
Eq.

S2.61
. Furthermore, we have the asymptotic expansion

P(z)N(z)^{-1}\sim\mathds{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{k}(z)\dfrac{1}{n^{k}},\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

(S2.74)

uniformly for
z
in compact subsets of
\{0<|z-1|<\delta_{2}\}
and
\sigma
in compact subsets of
(\pi/3,\pi)
, where
\Delta_{k}
is a meromorphic
2\times 2
matrix-valued function determined by
Eqs.

S2.57
,
S2.62
,
S2.72
,
S2.59
and
S2.68
, and given explicitly by

\Delta_{k}(z)=\dfrac{1}{2\left(-\phi_{n}(z)\right)^{k}}N(z)z^{(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{k}(s_{k}+t_{k})&i(s_{k}-t_{k})\\
-i(-1)^{k}(s_{k}-t_{k})&s_{k}+t_{k}\end{pmatrix}z^{-(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}}N(z)^{-1},

(S2.75)

where
\phi_{n}
is defined in
Eq.

S2.40
,
N(z)
is given in
Eq.

S2.53
, and the scalars
s_{k}
and
t_{k}
are defined in
Eq.

S2.60
. One can verify that
\Delta_{k}(z)
is meromorphic in a neighbourhood of
z=1
by using the jump conditions of
\phi_{n}(z)
and
N(z)
.

The first term in this asymptotic expansion is (suppressing
z
arguments to reduce notational clutter)

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}=\dfrac{-1}{144\phi_{n}}\Big[

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}+a^{-2}&2^{-\rho}i(a^{2}-a^{-2})\\
2^{\rho}i(a^{2}-a^{-2})&-(a^{2}+a^{-2})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle+3

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}(\varphi^{\rho}+\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})&2^{-\rho}i\left[\varphi^{\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}+\varphi^{-\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}\right]\\
2^{\rho}i\left[\varphi^{-\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}+\varphi^{\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}\right]&-(\varphi^{\rho}+\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})\end{pmatrix}\Big].

Note that
\Delta_{k}(z)
has a pole of order
(3k+1)/2
at
z=1
.

S2.9.5
Parametrix near
z=-1

We can use symmetry to carry over most of the analysis of the parametrix near
z=1
to obtain a parametrix near
z=-1
. In particular, consider the transformation

\widetilde{\Psi}(z)\coloneqq\sigma_{3}\Psi(-z)\sigma_{3}.

(S2.76)

Under the transformation
z\to-z
, the approach to a contour from the
(+)
-side gets mapped to the approach to the equivalent contour at
-z
but from the
(-)
-side. This implies that the corresponding jump matrix is the
inverse
of the jump matrix at
-z
. For the set of jump matrices considered here, the extra conjugation by
\sigma_{3}
is sufficient to correct for this inverse, i.e.
\sigma_{3}v_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{3}=v_{j}
, so that
\widetilde{\Psi}(z)
has the correct jump behaviour near
z=-1
.

We construct the rest of the parametrix in a similar way to before, with the result

P(z)=\widetilde{E}(z)\widetilde{\Psi}(-f_{n}(-z))e^{-n\phi_{n}(-z)\sigma_{3}}(-z)^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}},

(S2.77)

where
\widetilde{E}(z)
is an analytic matrix-valued function given by

\widetilde{E}(z)=N(z)(-z)^{(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(\pi i/4)\sigma_{3}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}f_{n}(-z)^{\sigma_{3}/4}.

(S2.78)

Notice the slight difference in the constant matrices in the definitions of
\widetilde{E}(z)
and
E(z)
, which comes about from conjugating with
\sigma_{3}
. Note that for
\rho=0
we simply have
P(z)=\sigma_{3}P(-z)\sigma_{3}
, but this is no longer quite true for
\rho\neq 0
, the reason being that the identity
\sigma_{3}N(z)\sigma_{3}=N(-z)
holds only for
\rho=0
.

As before, we have an asymptotic expansion

P(z)N^{-1}(z)\sim\mathds{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{\Delta}_{k}(z)\dfrac{1}{n^{k}},\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

(S2.79)

uniformly for
z
in compact subsets of
U_{\delta_{2}}\coloneqq\{0<|z+1|<\delta_{2}\}
and
\sigma
in compact subsets of
(\pi/3,\pi)
, where
\widetilde{\Delta}_{k}
is a meromorphic
2\times 2
matrix-valued function given by

\widetilde{\Delta}_{k}(z)=\dfrac{1}{2\left(-\phi_{n}(-z)\right)^{k}}N(z)(-z)^{(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{k}(s_{k}+t_{k})&-i(s_{k}-t_{k})\\
i(-1)^{k}(s_{k}-t_{k})&s_{k}+t_{k}\end{pmatrix}(-z)^{-(1/2)\rho\sigma_{3}}N(z)^{-1}.

(S2.80)

The first term in this expansion is

\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}(z)=\dfrac{-1}{144\phi_{n}(-z)}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}+a^{-2}&i2^{-\rho}(a^{2}-a^{-2})\\
i2^{\rho}(a^{2}-a^{-2})&-(a^{2}+a^{-2})\end{pmatrix}-3\Omega_{1}(z)\right],

(S2.81)

where
\Omega_{1}(z)
is defined for
U_{\delta_{2}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}
as

\Omega_{1}(z)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}(e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}+e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})&2^{-\rho}i\left[e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}+e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}\right]\\
2^{\rho}i\left[e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}+e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}\right]&-(e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}+e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})\end{pmatrix},

(S2.82)

and for
U_{\delta_{2}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{-}
as

\Omega_{1}(z)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}(e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}+e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})&2^{-\rho}i\left[e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}+e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}\right]\\
2^{\rho}i\left[e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho}(a+a^{-1})^{2}+e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}(a-a^{-1})^{2}\right]&-(e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi^{\rho}+e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi^{-\rho})(a^{2}-a^{-2})\end{pmatrix}.

(S2.83)

In these expressions we have suppressed for brevity the arguments of the functions
a
and
\varphi
, which are meant to be evaluated at
z
; the only function evaluated at
-z
is
\phi_{n}
.

S2.10
Local parametrix
P
near the origin

In this section we construct a local parametrix
P
which approximately solves the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
S
in a disk
U_{\delta_{n}}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<\delta_{n}\}
of radius
\delta_{n}=\gamma/\beta_{n}
centered at the origin. Our analysis will be inspired by that of
[
67
,
48
,
49
]
, but the main novelty will be that we construct the parametrix in a disk of radius shrinking like
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
. We do this to avoid having to deal with any singularities in the equilibrium measure; it was for this purpose that we assumed analyticity of the weight function in a region
C_{\theta}
which is invariant under rescaling, as well as in a disk of constant radius centered at the origin. The price we will pay for shrinking the disk is error bounds in the matching condition
P(z)N(z)^{-1}\sim\mathds{1}+o(1)
which decay more slowly with
n
.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
P

(P_{a})

P
is analytic in
U_{\delta_{n}}\setminus\Sigma
.

(P_{b})

P_{+}(z)=P_{-}(z)v_{S}(z)
for
z\in\Sigma\cap U_{\delta_{n}}
, with
v_{S}
the jump matrix for
S
.

(P_{c})

P(z)N(z)^{-1}\sim\mathds{1}+o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
z
on the boundary
\partial U_{\delta}
of the disk
U_{\delta}
for
\delta
in compact subsets of
(0,\delta_{n})
.

As in
Section

S2.9
, we will first transform to a problem
P^{(1)}
with constant jump matrices, and then construct an explicit solution to this problem using special functions.

S2.10.1
Transformation to constant jump matrices

Following Ref.
[
67
]
, in order to transform to constant jump matrices, we seek the parametrix
P
near
0
in the following form

P(z)=E_{n}(z)P^{(1)}(z)W(z)^{-\sigma_{3}}e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}},

(S2.84)

where
E_{n}(z)
is an analytic matrix-valued function to be determined to ensure the matching condition
(P_{c})
, and
W(z)
is defined by

W(z)\coloneqq\begin{cases}z^{\rho/2},&\text{if }\pi/2<\left|\arg{f_{n}(z)}\right|<\pi,\\
(-z)^{\rho/2},&\text{if }0<\left|\arg{f_{n}(z)}\right|<\pi/2,\end{cases}

(S2.85)

where
f_{n}:U_{\delta_{n}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})
is a biholomorphic map to be constructed in the next section. Note that
W(z)
has a branch cut along the whole real axis. One can then verify that
P^{(1)}
has a jump matrix which is piecewise constant
[
67
]
:

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=P^{(1)}_{-}(x)\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }x\in\Sigma_{1}^{o}\cup\Sigma_{5}^{o},

(S2.86)

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=P^{(1)}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-i\pi\rho}&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }z\in\Sigma_{2}^{o}\cup\Sigma_{6}^{o},

(S2.87)

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=P^{(1)}_{-}(z)e^{\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }z\in\Sigma_{3}^{o}\cup\Sigma_{7}^{o},

(S2.88)

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=P^{(1)}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{i\pi\rho}&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }z\in\Sigma_{4}^{o}\cup\Sigma_{8}^{o},

(S2.89)

where the contours
\Sigma_{j}^{o}
,
j=1,\dots,8
are sketched in
Fig.

S3
(a), with the understanding that the notation
\Sigma_{i}^{o}
denotes
\Sigma_{i}
without the origin. We construct an explicit solution for this RHP using a model solution
\Psi_{\rho/2}(\zeta)
for an auxiliary RHP in the
\zeta
-plane, as given in
[
67
,
48
]
and illustrated in
Fig.

S3
(b).

Figure S3:
(a)
The contour
\Sigma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{8}\Sigma_{i}
in the
z
-plane on which
P^{(1)}(z)
has jumps. We construct the parametrix in a disk
U_{\delta_{n}}
of radius
\delta_{n}
centered at the origin.
(b)
The contour
\Gamma_{\Psi}
for the auxiliary RHP in the
\zeta
-plane used to obtain the parametrix near the origin.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\Psi_{\rho/2}

(\Psi_{\rho/2,a})

\Psi_{\rho/2}
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma_{\Psi}
.

(\Psi_{\rho/2,b})

\Psi_{\rho/2}
satisfies the following jump relations on
\Gamma_{\Psi}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rho/2,+}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\rho/2,-}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }\zeta\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{5},

(S2.90)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rho/2,+}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\rho/2,-}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-i\pi\rho}&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }\zeta\in\Gamma_{2}\cup\Gamma_{6},

(S2.91)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rho/2,+}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\rho/2,-}(\zeta)e^{\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }\zeta\in\Gamma_{3}\cup\Gamma_{7},

(S2.92)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rho/2,+}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\rho/2,-}(\zeta)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
e^{i\pi\rho}&1\end{pmatrix},\hskip-80.00012pt

\displaystyle\text{for }\zeta\in\Gamma_{4}\cup\Gamma_{8}.

(S2.93)

(\Psi_{\rho/2,c})

For
\rho<0
,
\Psi_{\rho/2}
has the following behavior as
\zeta\to 0
:

\Psi_{\rho/2}(\zeta)=\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}|\zeta|^{\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{\rho/2}\\
|\zeta|^{\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{\rho/2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{as }\zeta\to 0.

(S2.94)

For
\rho\geq 0
,
\Psi_{\rho/2}
has the following behavior as
\zeta\to 0
:

\Psi_{\rho/2}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}|\zeta|^{\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}\\
|\zeta|^{\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}\end{pmatrix},&\text{as }\zeta\to 0\text{ with }\zeta\in\text{II, III, VI, VII};\\
\mathcal{O}\begin{pmatrix}|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}\\
|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}&|\zeta|^{-\rho/2}\end{pmatrix},&\text{as }\zeta\to 0\text{ with }\zeta\in\text{I, IV, V, VIII}.\end{cases}

(S2.95)

This RHP was solved in
[
48
, Eqs. (4.26)-(4.33)]
. It is built out of the modified Bessel functions
I_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}
,
K_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}
and the modified Hankel functions
H^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}
,
H^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}
. The explicit formula for
\Psi_{\rho/2}
in sector I is

\Psi_{\rho/2}(\zeta)=\dfrac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\zeta^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}H^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}(\zeta)&-iH^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}(\zeta)\\[10.00002pt]
H^{(2)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}(\zeta)&-iH^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}e^{-(\frac{\rho}{2}+\frac{1}{4})i\pi\sigma_{3}},\quad\text{for }0<\arg{\zeta}<\dfrac{\pi}{4},

(S2.96)

and the expressions in the other sectors can be obtained from this one by following the jumps given in
(\Psi_{\rho/2,b})
. As
\zeta\to\infty
in the first quadrant, we can use the asymptotic formulae
[
151
, 9.2.7–9.2.10]
for the Hankel functions to get the asymptotic expansion

\Psi_{\rho/2}(\zeta)\sim\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{i^{k}}{(2\zeta)^{k}}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1),k\right)&-i\left(\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1),k\right)\\[5.0pt]
-i(-1)^{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1),k\right)&\left(\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1),k\right)\end{pmatrix}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\sigma_{3}}e^{-\frac{i\pi\rho}{4}\sigma_{3}}e^{-i\zeta\sigma_{3}},

(S2.97)

uniformly in
\zeta
, where for
k=0
we define
(v,0)=1
and for
k\geq 1

(v,k)\coloneqq\dfrac{(4v^{2}-1)(4v^{2}-9)\cdots(4v^{2}-(2k-1)^{2})}{2^{2k}k!}.

(S2.98)

See Ref.
[
48
]
for expressions in the other quadrants.

We then define

P^{(1)}(z)\coloneqq\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z)),\quad\text{for }z\in U_{\delta_{n}}\setminus f_{n}^{-1}(\Gamma),

(S2.99)

where
f_{n}(z)
is a biholomorphic map to be constructed in the next section.

S2.10.2
Construction of biholomorphic map

Following Ref.
[
67
]
, we define the map
f_{n}:U_{\delta_{n}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})
by

f_{n}(z)\coloneqq\begin{cases}i\phi_{n}(z)-i\phi_{n,+}(0),&\text{if }\operatorname{Im}{z}>0,\\
-i\phi_{n}(z)-i\phi_{n,+}(0),&\text{if }\operatorname{Im}{z}<0,\end{cases}

(S2.100)

where
\phi_{n,+}(0)=i\pi/2
for an even weight function. From
Eqs.

S2.38
and
S2.40
we know that
\phi_{n}(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
(within its domain) and flips sign across
(-1,1)
, which implies that
f_{n}(z)
is analytic in a neighborhood of
z=0
. On the real line we have

f_{n}(x)=\pi\int_{0}^{x}\psi_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{for }x\in(-\delta_{n},\delta_{n}),

(S2.101)

which implies that
f_{n}^{\prime}(0)=\pi\psi_{n}(0)>0
. The behavior of
f_{n}(z)
near the origin is then given by

f_{n}(z)=\pi\psi_{n}(0)z+\mathcal{O}(z^{3}),\quad\text{as }z\to 0,

(S2.102)

where
f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
follows from the fact that
\psi_{n}(x)
is even. The big-
\mathcal{O}
hides potential
n
-dependence, which we will need to be more careful about in order to guarantee that
f_{n}(z)
is actually biholomorphic in a neighborhood of the origin.

Proposition S3
.

There exists a
\gamma>0
such that for
\delta_{n}=\gamma/\beta_{n}
and sufficiently large
n
,
f_{n}:U_{\delta_{n}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})
satisfies the following properties.

(a)

f_{n}:U_{\delta_{n}}\to f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})
is biholomorphic.

(b)

f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}}\cap\mathbb{R})=f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})\cap\mathbb{R}
,
f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm})=f_{n}(U_{\delta_{n}})\cap\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
.

Proof.

Property (b) will follow as in
Proposition

S2
. Regarding property (a), we have already established that
f_{n}
is analytic in a neighborhood of
z=0
, so it remains to show that it is invertible. To that end, for
|z|\leq r_{0}
we use Cauchy’s theorem for derivatives on a circle of radius
r_{1}>r_{0}
to give

\displaystyle\left|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\dfrac{2}{2\pi i}\oint_{|u|=r_{1}}\dfrac{f_{n}(u)}{(u-z)^{3}}\mathrm{d}u\right|,

(S2.103)

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2r_{1}}{(r_{1}-r_{0})^{3}}\sup_{|u|=r_{1}}|f_{n}(u)|.

(S2.104)

We are implicitly taking
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
to scale like
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, but we will not explicitly denote their
n
dependence. Now, from
Eqs.

S2.100
,
S2.40
and
S2.37
, we see that

\sup_{|u|=r_{1}}|f_{n}(u)|\leq\dfrac{r_{1}}{2}\sup_{|u|=r_{1}}\left|r(u)h_{n}(u)\right|.

(S2.105)

We show in
Eq.

S5.16
that, for sufficiently large
n
, we have
h_{n}(z)=h_{n}(0)[1+o(1)]
uniformly for
z
in a sufficiently small disk of radius
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
centered at the origin, where the
o(1)
refers to scaling with
n
. Combined with the fact that
r(u)=(u+1)^{1/2}(u-1)^{1/2}
is bounded near zero, for sufficiently large
n
we have

\sup_{|u|=r_{1}}|f_{n}(u)|\leq Cr_{1}h_{n}(0).

(S2.106)

for some constant
C>1/2
(independent of
n
). Inserting this into
Eq.

S2.104
gives

\left|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)\right|\leq 2C\dfrac{r_{1}^{2}}{(r_{1}-r_{0})^{3}}h_{n}(0),\quad\text{for }|z|\leq r_{0}<r_{1}.

(S2.107)

Again for
|z|\leq r_{0}
, we then have

\left|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-f_{n}^{\prime}(0)\right|=\left|\int_{0}^{z}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right|\leq 2C\dfrac{r_{0}r_{1}^{2}}{(r_{1}-r_{0})^{3}}h_{n}(0).

(S2.108)

We then set
r_{0}=r_{1}/N
for some large
N>1
, and use
f_{n}^{\prime}(0)=\pi\psi_{n}(0)=h_{n}(0)/2
, so that

\left|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-f_{n}^{\prime}(0)\right|\leq 4C\dfrac{N^{2}}{(N-1)^{3}}f_{n}^{\prime}(0).

(S2.109)

By taking
N
sufficiently large (but independent of
n
), we can make the RHS less than, say,
f_{n}^{\prime}(0)/2
, so we have a uniform (in
z
) nonzero lower bound on
|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|
in the disk of radius
r_{0}
. We can also make this uniform in
n
by using
Eq.

S5.2
to establish that
f_{n}^{\prime}(0)=h_{n}(0)/2
is
\Omega(1)
as
n\to\infty
. This is enough to conclude that
f_{n}(z)
is invertible and hence biholomorphic in a sufficiently small
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
neighborhood of the origin.
∎

S2.10.3
Satisfying the matching condition

In this final step, we construct the invertible analytic matrix-valued function
E(z)
to ensure that the matching function
(P_{c})
is satisfied. Following Ref.
[
67
]
, we define
E_{n}(z)
by

E_{n}(z)\coloneqq E(z)e^{n\phi_{n,+}(0)\sigma_{3}}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}\sigma_{3}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix},

(S2.110)

where the matrix valued function
E
is defined in different regions of the complex plane by

E(z)\coloneqq\begin{cases}N(z)W(z)^{\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{1}{4}\rho\pi i\sigma_{3}},&\text{for }z\in f_{n}^{-1}(\text{I}\cup\text{II}),\\
N(z)W(z)^{\sigma_{3}}e^{-\frac{1}{4}\rho\pi i\sigma_{3}},&\text{for }z\in f_{n}^{-1}(\text{III}\cup\text{IV}),\\
N(z)W(z)^{\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}e^{-\frac{1}{4}\rho\pi i\sigma_{3}},&\text{for }z\in f_{n}^{-1}(\text{V}\cup\text{VI}),\\
N(z)W(z)^{\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}e^{\frac{1}{4}\rho\pi i\sigma_{3}},&\text{for }z\in f_{n}^{-1}(\text{VII}\cup\text{VIII}).\end{cases}

(S2.111)

This ends the construction of the parametrix
P
near
z=0
.

S2.10.4
Summary of the local analysis near
z=0

We have defined the contour
\Sigma
in a shrinking disk
U_{\delta_{n}}
near
z=0
as the inverse image of the contour
\Gamma_{\Psi}
under the map
f_{n}
defined in
Eq.

S2.100
. We have then found a solution

P(z)=E_{n}(z)\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))W(z)^{-\sigma_{3}}e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}},\quad\text{for }z\in\Sigma\cap U_{\delta_{n}}

(S2.112)

to the RHP for
P
, where the matrix-valued function
E_{n}
is defined in
Eq.

S2.110
, the matrix-valued function
\Psi_{\rho/2}
is defined in
Eq.

S2.96
, and
W(z)
is defined in
Eq.

S2.85
. Furthermore, following Ref.
[
48
]
, on the boundary
\partial U_{\delta_{n}}
, we have the asymptotic expansion

P(z)N(z)^{-1}\sim\mathds{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{\Delta(k,n)(z)}{[nf_{n}(z)]^{k}},\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,\quad\text{for }z\in\partial U_{\delta_{n}},

(S2.113)

where the coefficient matrix
\Delta(k,n)(z)
is determined by
Eqs.

S2.112
,
S2.99
,
S2.97
,
S2.100
and
S2.110
, and given explicitly by

\Delta(k,n)(z)=\dfrac{i^{k}}{2^{k+1}}E(z)e^{n\phi_{n,+}(0)\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^{k}s_{\rho/2,k}&-t_{\rho/2,k}\\
-(-1)^{k}t_{\rho/2,k}&s_{\rho/2,k}\end{pmatrix}e^{-n\phi_{n,+}(0)\sigma_{3}}E(z)^{-1},

(S2.114)

and the constants
s_{\rho/2,k}
and
t_{\rho/2,k}
are defined as

s_{\rho/2,k}\coloneqq\left(\dfrac{1}{2}(\rho+1),k\right)+\left(\dfrac{1}{2}(\rho-1),k\right),\quad\text{and}\quad t_{\rho/2,k}\coloneqq\left(\dfrac{1}{2}(\rho+1),k\right)-\left(\dfrac{1}{2}(\rho-1),k\right),

(S2.115)

where the expression
(v,k)
was defined in
Eq.

S2.98
. For later, we note that

s_{\rho/2,1}=\dfrac{\rho^{2}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{\rho/2,1}=\rho.

(S2.116)

We will soon discuss this in more detail, but let us note two things about
\Delta(k,n)(z)
: first, that it is analytic in a neighbourhood of
z=0
, and second, that its only
n
dependence comes from the factors of
\exp[\pm n\phi_{n,+}(0)]=\exp[\pm n\pi i/2]
, where the value of
\phi_{n,+}(0)
is fixed by the even symmetry of the weight function. Since this factor is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, the asymptotic
n
-decay of
P(z)N(z)^{-1}-\mathds{1}
will be determined solely by the factors of
[nf_{n}(z)]^{k}
in
Eq.

S2.113
.

Finally, in the case
\rho=0
, this whole construction reduces to
P(z)=N(z)
exactly. This will mean that
R(z)
has no jump near the origin if
\rho=0
.

S2.11
Riemann-Hilbert problem for
R

Having constructed local parametrices
P(z)
in disks centered at
z=0
and
z=\pm 1
, we define

S_{\mathrm{par}}(z)\coloneqq\begin{cases}P(z),&\text{if }|z-1|<\delta_{2}\text{ or }|z+1|<\delta_{2}\text{ or }|z|<\delta_{n},\\
N(z),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(S2.117)

Now we define

R(z)\coloneqq S(z)S_{\mathrm{par}}^{-1}(z).

(S2.118)

One can verify that
R(z)
is the unique solution of the following RHP, with jumps on the contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}
sketched in
Fig.

S4
.

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{8}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{9}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{2}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{3}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{4}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7}
-1
1
0

Figure S4:
The contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{9}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j}
on which
R(z)
has jumps. The circles
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=5,6,7}
are determined in the construction of the local parametrices in
Sections

S2.9
and
S2.10
; note that
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
has radius
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, so as
n\to\infty
it is much smaller than
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6,7}
which have
\mathcal{O}(1)
radius. The precise shape of the lens boundaries
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}
is given in
Fig.

S6
.

Riemann-Hilbert problem for
R

(R_{a})

R:\mathbb{C}\setminus\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is analytic.

(R_{b})

R
has the following jump matrices.

R_{+}(s)=R_{-}(s)\begin{cases}N(s)v_{i}(s)N^{-1}(s),&\text{for }s\in\widetilde{\Sigma}_{i},i=1,2,3,4,8,9;\\
P(s)N^{-1}(s),&\text{for }s\in\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}\cup\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}\cup\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7};\\
\mathds{1},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(S2.119)

Here
v_{i}(s)
is the jump matrix for
S
on the relevant contour (see
Eqs.

S2.46
,
S2.47
and
S2.48
).

(R_{c})

R(z)=\mathds{1}+\mathcal{O}(1/|z|)
as
|z|\to\infty
.

In particular, the jump matrix
v_{R}(z)
of
R
is uniformly close to the identity, in the sense that, with
\Delta_{R}(z)\coloneqq v_{R}(z)-\mathds{1}
, as
n\to\infty
we have
\lVert\Delta_{R}(z)\rVert_{L_{\infty}(\widetilde{\Sigma})}\to 0
. In more detail, we have

\begin{gathered}\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}\right)}=o\left(\dfrac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})}\right),\quad\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}\right)}=o\left(\dfrac{1}{n^{1/2}\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})}\right),\quad\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{1}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}\right)}=o\left(\dfrac{1}{n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})}\right),\\
\begin{aligned} &\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{8,9}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-cn}\right),\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} &\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}^{1/2}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\\
&\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}\left(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{8,9}\right)}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-cn}\right),\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(S2.120)

where
c>0
is a positive
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant. When
\beta_{n}
is sublinear in
n
, the bounds on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
are polynomially small in
n
. In the marginal case
p=1
where
\beta_{n}\sim\mathcal{O}(n/\log^{q}{n})
, the
L_{\infty}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5})
bound decays polylogarithmically in
n
provided
q>-1
, given the logarithmic divergence
h_{n}(0)=(\log{n})^{1+o(1)}
of the equilibrium measure proved in
Eq.

S5.2
. The precise rate of convergence for
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}
depends on
p
and
q
; for
p>1
the convergence is actually superpolynomial, while for
p=1
the convergence may be superpolynomial or subpolynomial (but still super-polylogarithmic) depending on whether
q>0
or
q\leq 0
(see the proof of
Eq.

S5.63
for details).

The bounds on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6,7,8,9}
are standard; see
[
62
]
. The
L_{\infty}
bound on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
follows from the fact that
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
has a radius of
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, combined with the asymptotic expansion
Eq.

S2.113
, the definition
Eq.

S2.100
of
f_{n}(z)
, and the uniform lower bound for
h_{n}(z)
given in
Eq.

S5.16
. The
L_{2}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5})
bound follows from the
L_{\infty}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5})
bound by Hölder’s inequality. The
L_{\infty}
bound on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}
follows from the fact that the matrix elements of
\Delta_{R}(z)
are proportional to
e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}D(z)^{2}\omega(z)^{-1}
on the lens boundaries, and
D(z)^{2}\omega(z)^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(|z|^{0})
as
z\to 0
, while
|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
super-polylogarithmically in
n
by
Eq.

S5.63
. The
L_{1,2}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4})
bounds follows from the second part of
Eq.

S5.63
.

S2.11.1
Solution for
R

We have constructed
R(z)
such that its jumps are ‘small’, in the sense that its jump matrix
v_{R}(z)
is uniformly close to the identity as
n\to\infty
. This will allow us to write down its solution using standard techniques (see
[
62
, Appendix A]
). For
f\in L_{2}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R})
, we can define the Cauchy-Stieltjes transform

(Cf)(z)\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}}\dfrac{f(s)}{s-z}\mathrm{d}s,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}.

(S2.121)

We denote by
(C_{\pm}f)(z)
the limiting functions as
z
approaches
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}
from the
\pm
sides. Now with
\Delta_{R}(z)\coloneqq v_{R}(z)-\mathds{1}
, we define for matrix-valued functions
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
the weighted Cauchy-Stieltjes transform

C_{\Delta_{R}}f\coloneqq C_{-}(f\Delta_{R}).

(S2.122)

Now, by the discussion in the previous section, for
n\to\infty
we have
\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{\infty}}\to 0
, which by the boundedness of the Cauchy-Stieltjes transform implies that the
L_{2}
-operator norm
\lVert C_{\Delta_{R}}\rVert_{L_{2}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then for sufficiently large
n
, the operator
\mathrm{Id}-C_{\Delta_{R}}
can be inverted by a Neumann series, and so we can employ the standard solution
[
62
]

R(z)=\mathds{1}+C\left(\Delta_{R}+\mu_{R}\Delta_{R}\right)(z),\quad\text{where }\mu_{R}\coloneqq\left(\mathrm{Id}-C_{\Delta_{R}}\right)^{-1}C_{-}\Delta_{R},

(S2.123)

Expanding
R(z)=\mathds{1}+R_{1}/z+\cdots
, this formula gives

R_{1}=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}}\left[\Delta_{R}(y)+\mu_{R}(y)\Delta_{R}(y)\right]\mathrm{d}y.

(S2.124)

From the definition of
\mu_{R}
, we have
\lVert\mu_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}}\leq c\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}}
for some
c>0
by the boundedness of the Cauchy-Stieltjes transform, and hence by Cauchy-Schwarz we have

R_{1}=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}}\Delta_{R}(y)\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}\left(\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R})}^{2}\right).

(S2.125)

S2.11.2
Error bounds for
R

By construction, we have
R(z)\to\mathds{1}
as
n\to\infty
. In this section we give the rate of this convergence, which follows from combining
Eq.

S2.123
with
Eq.

S2.120
. In order to deal with points near the contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, one can employ a contour deformation argument as in the proof of
[
62
, Corollary 7.9]
. The sitation is most delicate for
z
near the origin. For
|z|\leq\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, we have the bound

\lVert R(z)-\mathds{1}\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right),\mathclap{\hskip 150.00023pt\text{uniformly for }|z|\leq\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n}),}

(S2.126)

with the dominant contribution coming from integrating over the contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
. Again, this may decay only polylogarithmically in the worst case
p=1
,
q>-1
. For larger
|z|
, similar arguments using
Eq.

S2.120
lead to

\lVert R(z)-\mathds{1}\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}|z|}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\mathclap{\hskip 100.00015pt\text{for }|z|\geq\Omega(1/\beta_{n}),}

(S2.127)

where the factor of
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n}|z|)
gets cut off at
\mathcal{O}(1)
for
|z|\sim\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, and the factor of
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
comes from the contours
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6,7}
. The proof of
Eq.

S5.63
shows that the contribution from the lens boundaries
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1..4}
is
o(1/n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n}))
and so is subleading.

One can similarly establish bounds on the derivatives: near the origin we have

\lVert R^{(k)}(z)\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\beta_{n}^{k}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right),\mathclap{\hskip 150.00023pt\text{uniformly for }|z|\leq\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n}),}

(S2.128)

and for larger
z
this gets replaced with

\lVert R^{(k)}(z)\rVert\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{|z|^{k}}\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}|z|}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{nh_{n}(0)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\right),\mathclap{\hskip 100.00015pt\text{for }|z|\geq\Omega(1/\beta_{n}).}

(S2.129)

Remark 1
.

Note that these bounds imply that the derivatives of
R(z)
could in principle grow with
n
, in contrast to the more standard situation where all derivatives of
R(z)
are bounded
[
62
,
67
]
. This is a consequence of shrinking the contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
like
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
, which was necessitated by our fairly weak analyticity assumptions on
Q
. One might worry that these growing derivatives could dominate asymptotics for the Christoffel-Darboux (CD) kernel, which involves taking derivatives of
Y(z)
(see
Section

S4
). However, the contributions from
R
derivatives turn out to still be subleading relative to the derivatives of leading terms, since those derivatives themselves bring factors which grow with
n
. To illustrate this, a comparison one has to make for CD asymptotics in the bulk is between
\lVert R^{\prime}(x)\rVert
and
n\phi_{n,+}^{\prime}(x)
, the latter coming from differentiating
e^{-n\phi_{n,+}(x)\sigma_{3}}
. From
Eq.

S2.41
we have

n\phi_{n,+}^{\prime}(x)=-i\pi\,n\psi_{n}(x)=\dfrac{-i\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}{2}nh_{n}(x).

(S2.130)

In the bulk
\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
is bounded away from 0, so one can then use the uniform lower bounds on
h_{n}(x)
available from
Lemmas

S6
,
S5.16
and
3
to see that
n\phi_{n,+}^{\prime}(x)
dominates over
\lVert R^{\prime}(x)\rVert
as estimated from
Eq.

S2.129
. Similar conclusions can be made for asymptotics in the Bessel and Airy regions near
z=0
and
z=\pm 1
respectively.

S3
Extracting the recurrence coefficients: proof of Theorem 1

S3.1
Reversing the transformations

We have made a series of transformations from the original Riemann-Hilbert problem:

Y\mapsto U\mapsto T\mapsto S\mapsto R.

We will now reverse these transformations, with the goal of extracting the
z
-dependence of
Y(z)
as
z\to\infty
.

Recall that
U(z)=\beta_{n}^{-(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}Y(\beta_{n}z)\beta_{n}^{(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}
. Making the expansions
U(z)=(\mathds{1}+U_{1}/z+\cdots)z^{n\sigma_{3}}
and
Y(z)=(\mathds{1}+Y_{1}/z+\cdots)z^{n\sigma_{3}}
, linear independence gives

Y_{1}=\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}U_{1}\beta_{n}^{-(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}.

(S3.1)

Next we recall that
U(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}T(z)e^{-\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}e^{ng(z)\sigma_{3}}
. Expanding
T_{1}=\mathds{1}+T_{1}/z+\cdots
and
e^{ng(z)\sigma_{3}}=(\mathds{1}+G_{1}/z+\cdots)z^{n\sigma_{3}}
, we get

U_{1}=e^{\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}+G_{1},

(S3.2)

and hence

\displaystyle Y_{1}=

\displaystyle\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}\sigma_{3}}\beta_{n}^{-(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}

(S3.3)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}G_{1}\beta_{n}^{-(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}.

Note that, since
G_{1}
came from the expansion of
e^{ng(z)\sigma_{3}}
, which is a diagonal matrix,
G_{1}
itself is also diagonal, and hence will not contribute to the off-diagonal elements of
Y_{1}
, which are the ones that matter for the recurrence coefficients.

For the
T\to S
transformation, we simply have
T_{1}=S_{1}
, since
S
differs from
T
only for small values of
z
. Finally, for the
S\to R
transformation, we have
S(z)=R(z)N(z)
for
z\to\infty
, and hence

S_{1}=R_{1}+N_{1}.

(S3.4)

Substituting into the formula for
Y_{1}
and using
b_{n}^{2}=(Y_{1})_{12}(Y_{1})_{21}
, we have

b_{n}^{2}=\beta_{n}^{2}(R_{1}+N_{1})_{12}(R_{1}+N_{1})_{21}.

(S3.5)

Using the
z\to\infty
asymptotics for
N(z)
, we get

b_{n}=\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2}\left(1+2i\left[2^{-\rho}(R_{1})_{21}-2^{\rho}(R_{1})_{12}\right]+4(R_{1})_{12}(R_{1})_{21}\right)^{1/2}.

(S3.6)

Note that the leading order behaviour is given by
b_{n}\simeq\beta_{n}/2
, since
R_{1}
is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
. From the solution for
R(z)
, we have

2^{-\rho}(R_{1})_{21}-2^{\rho}(R_{1})_{12}=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}}\left[2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)+\cdots\right]\mathrm{d}y.

(S3.7)

The
(R_{1})_{12}(R_{1})_{21}
term in the formula for
b_{n}
will be subleading relative to this term because it is quadratic in
R_{1}
.

S3.2
Recurrence coefficients from residue calculus

Next we use the asymptotic expansions of
\Delta_{R}
constructed in the various local parametrices to evaluate the RHS of
Eq.

S3.7
using the residue theorem.

S3.2.1
Integral over the circular contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
near
z=0

Since
\Delta_{R}(z)=P(z)N(z)^{-1}-\mathds{1}
on this contour, taking the first term of the asymptotic expansion
Eq.

S2.113
and using
[a(z)+a(z)^{-1}]^{2}-[a(z)-a(z)^{-1}]^{2}=4
, we get

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)=(-1)^{n+1}\dfrac{i\rho}{4n}\dfrac{1}{f_{n}(z)}\begin{cases}\left(e^{\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}}\varphi(z)^{-\rho}+e^{-\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}}\varphi(z)^{\rho}\right),&z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\\
\left(e^{\frac{-i\pi\rho}{2}}\varphi(z)^{-\rho}+e^{\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}}\varphi(z)^{\rho}\right),&z\in\mathbb{C}_{-}.\end{cases}

(S3.8)

Near
z=0
, we have

f_{n}(z)=\pi\psi_{n}(0)z+\mathcal{O}(z^{3}).

(S3.9)

However,
\varphi(z)
has a branch cut along the real axis, with

\varphi(z)=\begin{cases}i+\mathcal{O}(z),&z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\\
-i+\mathcal{O}(z),&z\in\mathbb{C}_{-}.\end{cases}

(S3.10)

This cancels out the differing phase factors in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
, so we get the Laurent expansion

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)=(-1)^{n+1}\dfrac{i\rho}{2n}\dfrac{1}{\pi\psi_{n}(0)}\dfrac{1}{z}+\mathcal{O}(z^{0}),

(S3.11)

valid in both
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. Then the contour integral gives

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}}\left[2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)+\cdots\right]\mathrm{d}y=(-1)^{n}i\rho\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(0)}+\cdots,

(S3.12)

where we note that
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{5}
is defined with clockwise orientation, and we have used
\psi_{n}(0)=h_{n}(0)/2\pi
.

S3.2.2
Integral over the circular contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}
near
z=1

Since
\Delta_{R}(z)=P(z)N(z)^{-1}-\mathds{1}
on this contour, taking the first term of the asymptotic expansion
Eq.

S2.74
, we have

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)=-\dfrac{i}{12n\phi_{n}(z)}\left[\varphi(z)^{\rho}-\varphi(z)^{-\rho}\right].

(S3.13)

Near
z=1
, we have

\varphi(z)^{\rho}-\varphi(z)^{-\rho}=2\sqrt{2}\rho(z-1)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}[(z-1)^{3/2}].

(S3.14)

Since
\phi_{n}(z)\sim\mathcal{O}[(z-1)^{3/2}]
near
z=1
, the overall expression therefore has a simple pole at
z=1
. Recalling that
\phi_{n}(z)=-(2/3)(z-1)^{3/2}\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
, with
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(z)
analytic near
z=1
, we get

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)=\dfrac{i\rho}{2}\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(1)}\dfrac{1}{z-1}+\mathcal{O}(z^{0}),

(S3.15)

where we have used
\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)=h_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2}
. Therefore the contour integral gives

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}}\left[2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)+\cdots\right]\mathrm{d}y=-\dfrac{i\rho}{2}\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(1)}+\cdots,

(S3.16)

where again we take into account the clockwise orientation of
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{6}
. Note that
Eq.

57
tells us that
h_{n}(1)\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
as
n\to\infty
, so this contribution scales as
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
.

S3.2.3
Integral over the circular contour
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7}
near
z=-1

Similarly, taking into account the different expressions for
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}(z)
in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
from
Eq.

S2.79
, we get

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)=\dfrac{i}{12n\phi_{n}(-z)}\left[e^{\mp i\pi\rho}\varphi(z)^{\rho}-e^{\pm i\pi\rho}\varphi(z)^{-\rho}\right].

(S3.17)

To find the residue, we exploit analyticity and evaluate this expression as
z\to x+i0^{+}
with
x<-1
. This gives

\varphi(x)^{\rho}=e^{i\pi\rho}\left(1+\sqrt{2}\rho(-x-1)^{1/2}+\cdots\right).

(S3.18)

Then we have

e^{-i\pi\rho}\varphi(x)^{\rho}-e^{i\pi\rho}\varphi(x)^{-\rho}=2\sqrt{2}\rho(-x-1)^{1/2}+\cdots.

(S3.19)

Then again using
\phi_{n}(-z)=-(2/3)(-z-1)^{3/2}\hat{\varphi}_{n}(-z)
, we get

2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(x)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(x)=\dfrac{i\rho}{2}\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(1)}\dfrac{1}{x+1}+\mathcal{O}(x^{0}),

(S3.20)

so the residue is
i\rho/2nh_{n}(1)
. Hence the contour integral gives

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{7}}\left[2^{\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{12}(y)-2^{-\rho}(\Delta_{R})_{21}(y)+\cdots\right]\mathrm{d}y=-\dfrac{i\rho}{2}\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(1)}+\cdots,

(S3.21)

so to leading order we get the same contribution as from the circle around
z=1
.

S3.2.4
Contribution from the lens boundaries
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{j=1..4}

Let us focus on the lens boundary in the first quadrant,
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}
, with the analysis for the other lens boundaries following similarly. From
Eqs.

S2.118
and
S2.46
we have

\Delta_{R}(z)=e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}\omega(z)^{-1}N(z)\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}N(z)^{-1}.

(S3.22)

By using the solution
Eq.

S2.53
for
N(z)
, one can check that the factor following
e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}
is
\mathcal{O}(|z|^{0})
as
z\to\infty
. Then we have

\left|\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}}\Delta_{R}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\left|\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}}|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}|\mathrm{d}z\right|\right)

(S3.23)

In
Eq.

S5.63
we show that as
n\to\infty
the RHS is
o(1/n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n}))
, so that the contribution from the lens boundary is subleading compared with the contributions from the local parametrices at
z=0
and
z=\pm 1
(c.f.
Eqs.

S3.12
,
S3.16
and
S3.21
).

S3.2.5
Combining the contributions

With the dominant contributions from the circular contours from the local parametrices, we get

b_{n}=\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2}\left(1+2\rho\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(1)}-(-1)^{n}2\rho\dfrac{1}{nh_{n}(0)}+\cdots\right)^{1/2}.

(S3.24)

Expanding the square root gives

\boxed{b_{n}=\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2}\left(1+\rho\left[\dfrac{1}{h_{n}(1)}-(-1)^{n}\dfrac{1}{h_{n}(0)}\right]\dfrac{1}{n}+\cdots\right),}

(S3.25)

which concludes the proof of
Theorem

1
. The leading error term encoded in the dots depends on the growth exponents
p
and
q
of the potential
Q(x)
. For
p>1
, the leading error is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/n^{2-1/p})
, with equal order contributions from the
k=2
term in the asymptotic expansion of the
z=0
local parametrix, and from the
\mathcal{O}(\lVert\Delta_{R}\rVert_{L_{2}(\Sigma_{5})}^{2})
error in
Eq.

S2.125
. For
p=1
, this error reduces to
\mathcal{O}(1/n(\log{n})^{2+q+o(1)})
, which is subleading relative to the
1/nh_{n}(0)\sim 1/n(\log{n})^{1+o(1)}
term because we are assuming
q>-1
if
p=1
.

As a check of the theorem, we can consider the generalized Hermite polynomials, which have weight function
\Phi(x)/2\pi=|x|^{\rho}\exp[-x^{2}]
. With
Q(x)=x^{2}
, we have
\beta_{n}=\sqrt{2n}
, and
h_{n}(0)=h_{n}(1)=4
(c.f.
Lemmas

2
and
57
with
p=2
). Substituting into
Theorem

1
, we get agreement to
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
with the exact recurrence coefficients, which are known to be
b_{n}=(1/\sqrt{2})\sqrt{n+\frac{1}{2}[1-(-1)^{n}]\rho}

[
130
]
.

S3.3
Scaling of the leading coefficient
y_{n}

The leading coefficients
y_{n}>0
of the polynomial
p_{n}(x)=y_{n}x^{n}+\cdots
can be extracted from the solution
Y(z)
to the fundamental Riemann-Hilbert problem using
Eq.

S2.6
, which requires the matrix element
(Y_{1})_{12}
. Reversing the transformations for
Y
as before gives

(Y_{1})_{12}=\beta_{n}^{2n+1+\rho}e^{nl_{n}}\left(i2^{-(1+\rho)}+(R_{1})_{12}\right).

(S3.26)

The matrix element
(R_{1})_{12}
can be derived from a residue calculation like that for
b_{n}
, and takes the value

(R_{1})_{12}=\dfrac{1}{n}\dfrac{i}{2^{1+\rho}}\left[\dfrac{\rho}{h_{n}(0)}\left(\dfrac{\rho}{2}-(-1)^{n}\right)+\dfrac{1}{12h_{n}(1)}\left(2(2+6\rho+3\rho^{2})-3\dfrac{h_{n}^{\prime}(1)}{h_{n}(1)}\right)\right]+\cdots.

(S3.27)

The contribution to
(Y_{1})_{12}
from
(R_{1})_{12}
is therefore subleading by a factor of
1/n
compared with the factor
i2^{-(1+\rho)}
coming from
(N_{1})_{12}
. Thus we have

(Y_{1})_{12}=\dfrac{i}{2^{1+\rho}}\beta_{n}^{2n}\beta_{n}^{1+\rho}e^{nl_{n}}\left[1+\mathcal{O}(1/n)\right].

(S3.28)

Then the leading coefficient
y_{n}
scales like

\boxed{y_{n}=\dfrac{2^{\rho/2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\beta_{n}^{-n}\beta_{n}^{-(1+\rho)/2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}nl_{n}}\left[1+\mathcal{O}(1/n)\right].}

(S3.29)

By
Eq.

S1.35
, we know that the Lagrange multiplier
l_{n}
will be
\mathcal{O}(1)
and negative as
n\to\infty
. On the other hand,
\beta_{n}\sim n^{1/p}
, so the leading order scaling of
y_{n}
will be dictated by the factor
\beta_{n}^{-n}
, such that

y_{n}\sim e^{-\frac{1}{p}n\log{n}+\mathcal{O}(n)}.

(S3.30)

As a simple check of this scaling, we note that
y_{n}
is related to the recurrence coefficients by

y_{n}=(b_{1}b_{2}\cdots b_{n})^{-1}.

(S3.31)

Since the recurrence coefficients scale to leading order like
b_{n}\sim\beta_{n}
, this validates the factorial-like scaling
y_{n}\sim\beta_{n}^{-n}
. In Ref.
[
11
]
, by counting Dyck paths they give a lower bound for the moments

\mu_{2n}\geq y_{n}^{-2},

(S3.32)

so our result gives

\mu_{2n}\geq e^{\frac{2}{p}n\log{n}+\mathcal{O}(n)},

(S3.33)

so the moments grow at least as fast as a power of a factorial to leading order. Of course, this moment bound is immediate from just taking
b_{n}\sim n^{1/p}
, but our more precise estimate of
y_{n}
will be relevant in the next section.

S4
Asymptotics of the orthogonal polynomials

In this section we will use our constructed asymptotic solution of the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
Y(z)
to obtain the
n\to\infty
asymptotics of the orthogonal polynomials
p_{n}(z)
, as well as the associated Christoffel-Darboux (CD) kernel
K_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}p_{m}(x)p_{m}(y)
. From
Eq.

S2.4
, the polynomials
p_{n}(z)
and
p_{n-1}(z)
will be determined by the first column of
Y(z)
via

\displaystyle p_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=y_{n}Y_{11}(z),

(S4.1)

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(z)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{-1}{2\pi i}\dfrac{1}{y_{n}b_{n}}Y_{21}(z),

(S4.2)

where the recurrence coefficient
b_{n}
and the leading coefficient
y_{n}>0
of
p_{n}(x)=y_{n}x^{n}+\cdots
are determined from
Y_{1}
as in
Eqs.

S2.5
and
S2.6
.

For the CD kernel, the Christoffel-Darboux formula gives

\displaystyle K_{n}(x,t)=b_{n}\dfrac{p_{n}(x)p_{n-1}(t)-p_{n-1}(x)p_{n}(t)}{x-t},

(S4.3)

Again, we can evaluate this in terms of the matrix elements of the solution
Y
to the Riemann-Hilbert problem:

K_{n}(x,t)=\dfrac{-1}{2\pi i}\dfrac{Y_{11}(x)Y_{21}(t)-Y_{21}(x)Y_{11}(t)}{x-t}.

(S4.4)

We will mainly be interested in the diagonal case
t=x
, where we get

\displaystyle K_{n}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{-1}{2\pi i}\left(Y_{11}^{\prime}(x)Y_{21}(x)-Y_{21}^{\prime}(x)Y_{11}(x)\right),

(S4.5)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\det\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(x)&Y_{11}^{\prime}(x)\\
Y_{21}(x)&Y_{21}^{\prime}(x)\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.6)

S4.1
Behavior near the origin

Here we discuss the asymptotics near the origin
z=0
, where the scaling will be determined in terms of Bessel functions, by virtue of the local parametrix
\Psi_{\rho/2}
constructed in
Section

S2.10.1
. Following Ref.
[
67
]
, we reverse the transformations
Y\mapsto U\mapsto T\mapsto S\mapsto R
for
z\to 0
in the upper lens, in the region enclosed by the local parametrix near the origin. Combining
Eqs.

S2.7
,
S2.34
,
S2.45
,
S2.112
,
S2.117
and
S2.118
, this gives

\displaystyle Y(\beta_{n}z)

\displaystyle=\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(z)E_{n}(z)\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))z^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(i\pi\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}

(S4.7)

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
z^{-\rho}e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}&1\end{pmatrix}e^{-(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{ng_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}\beta_{n}^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}},

where we are taking
z
to be
\mathcal{O}(1/\beta_{n})
. The first column of
Y
is then given by

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{21}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=(\beta_{n}z)^{-\rho/2}e^{n\left[g_{n}(z)-\phi_{n}(z)-(l_{n}/2)\right]}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(z)E_{n}(z)\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))e^{(i\pi\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.8)

For
z
in the sector
f_{n}^{-1}(I)
, we can combine
Eq.

S2.96
with the Bessel function identities 9.1.3 and 9.1.4 of Ref.
[
151
]
to get

\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))e^{(i\pi\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}=e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{\pi}\left(nf_{n}(z)\right)^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}\left(nf_{n}(z)\right)\\
J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\left(nf_{n}(z)\right)\end{pmatrix},

(S4.9)

where
J_{\alpha}
is a Bessel function of the first kind. Sending
z\downarrow x\in(0,\delta_{n})
,
Lemmas

S7
and
S2.42
tell us that

e^{n\left[g_{n,+}(x)-\phi_{n,+}(x)-(l_{n}/2)\right]}=e^{\frac{n}{2}\left[g_{n,+}(x)+g_{n,-}(x)-l_{n}\right]}=e^{\frac{n}{2}V_{n}(x)}=e^{\frac{1}{2}Q(\beta_{n}x)},

(S4.10)

where we used
2\phi_{n,+}(x)=g_{n,+}(x)-g_{n,-}(x)
. We therefore get

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(\beta_{n}x)\\
Y_{21}(\beta_{n}x)\end{pmatrix}=e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{\pi}\beta_{n}^{-\rho/2}e^{\frac{1}{2}Q(\beta_{n}x)}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(x)E_{n}(x)\left(nf_{n}(x)\right)^{1/2}x^{-\rho/2}\begin{pmatrix}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}\left(nf_{n}(x)\right)\\
J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\left(nf_{n}(x)\right)\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.11)

Since by construction
R(x)=\mathds{1}+o(1)
, where the
o(1)
refers to scaling with
n
, for large
n
we can approximate
R(x)\approx\mathds{1}
. Then using the definition
Eq.

S2.110
of
E_{n}(x)
, we can unpack this formula to get the explicit expressions (temporarily suppressing some function arguments to reduce notation)

\displaystyle Y_{11}(\beta_{n}x)\approx\dfrac{\pi e^{-i\pi/4}}{2^{3/2}}\sqrt{\dfrac{nf_{n}(x)}{w(\beta_{n}x)}}\beta_{n}^{n+\rho/2}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}}2^{-\rho/2}\Bigg[

\displaystyle e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)

(S4.12)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)\Bigg],

\displaystyle Y_{21}(\beta_{n}x)\approx\dfrac{\pi e^{-i\pi/4}}{2^{3/2}}\sqrt{\dfrac{nf_{n}(x)}{w(\beta_{n}x)}}i\beta_{n}^{-(n+\rho/2)}e^{-\frac{nl_{n}}{2}}2^{\rho/2}\Bigg[

\displaystyle e^{\frac{-i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)

(S4.13)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)\Bigg],

with
w(x)=x^{\rho}e^{-Q(x)}
, and where the functions
a\equiv a(x)
and
\varphi\equiv\varphi(x)
are defined in
Eqs.

S2.54
and
S2.52
. In this region their
+
side expressions are
a_{+}(x)=e^{i\pi/4}(1-x)^{1/4}/(1+x)^{1/4}
and
\varphi_{+}(x)=x+i\sqrt{1-x^{2}}=e^{i\arccos(x)}
. The Bessel functions
J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}
should be evaluated with the argument
nf_{n}(x)
.

S4.1.1
Proof of
Eq.

76

With these expressions for the first column of
Y
near the origin, we can now get expressions for the orthogonal polynomials
p_{n}(x)
and
p_{n-1}(x)
using
Eqs.

S4.1
and
S4.2
, as well as the expression
Eq.

S3.29
for the leading coefficient
y_{n}
. For example, for
p_{n}
we get

\displaystyle p_{n}(\beta_{n}x)\approx\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2^{3/2}}\sqrt{\dfrac{nf_{n}(x)}{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)}}\Bigg[

\displaystyle e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)

(S4.14)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)\Bigg],

where the arguments of
\varphi_{+}\equiv\varphi_{+}(x)
,
a_{+}\equiv a_{+}(x)
and
J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}\equiv J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho\pm 1)}(nf_{n}(x))
are suppressed as before. For
\rho=0
, this reduces to
Eq.

99
of the main text after using
nf_{n}(x)=\pi I_{n}(\beta_{n}x)
, with
I_{n}
defined in
Eq.

97
. By the discussion surrounding
Eq.

S2.126
, this expression has a multiplicative error of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))
.

Using
f_{n}(x)=\pi\int_{0}^{x}\psi_{n}(s)\mathrm{d}s\approx\pi\psi_{n}(0)x
, we can take the limit
x\to 0^{+}
, giving

\displaystyle p_{n}(0)

\displaystyle\approx\cos\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{2}\right)\dfrac{2^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}}}{\Gamma\left[\frac{1+\rho}{2}\right]}e^{Q(0)/2}\dfrac{\left[\pi\sigma_{n}(0)\right]^{\rho/2}}{\beta_{n}^{1/2}},

(S4.15)

\displaystyle p_{n}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle\approx\sin\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{2}\right)\dfrac{2^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}}}{\Gamma\left[\frac{3+\rho}{2}\right]}e^{Q(0)/2}\dfrac{\left[\pi\sigma_{n}(0)\right]^{1+\rho/2}}{\beta_{n}^{1/2}}\left(1+\dfrac{(\rho+1)^{2}}{2\pi\beta_{n}\sigma_{n}(0)}\right),

(S4.16)

where
\sigma_{n}(0)=n\psi_{n}(0)/\beta_{n}
is the equilibrium density of the Coulomb gas at
\omega=0
. This proves
Eq.

76
in the main text. As a sanity check, we can see that
p_{n}(0)
vanishes for odd
n
and
p_{n}^{\prime}(0)
vanishes for even
n
. One can also check using
Eq.

S2.128
that the contribution from the derivative
R^{\prime}(z)
is subleading relative to the leading term by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))
(c.f.
Remark

1
), so the derivative
p_{n}^{\prime}(0)
has the same multiplicative error as
p_{n}(0)
.

Similarly, using
Eqs.

S4.2
,
S4.13
and
S3.29
, for
p_{n-1}
we get

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(\beta_{n}x)\approx-\dfrac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2^{3/2}}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2b_{n}}\sqrt{\dfrac{nf_{n}(x)}{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)}}\Bigg[
