\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)

(S4.17)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\left(e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}\right)\Bigg],

with the same argument suppression as before. Note that
Theorem

1
tells us that the factor
\beta_{n}/2b_{n}=1+\mathcal{O}(1/n)
; for
\rho=0
, this equation reduces to
Eq.

98
of the main text after approximating
\beta_{n}/2b_{n}\approx 1
.

Keeping
\rho
general, again taking the limit
x\to 0^{+}
gives

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(0)

\displaystyle\approx\sin\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{2}\right)\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2b_{n}}\dfrac{2^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}}}{\Gamma\left[\frac{1+\rho}{2}\right]}e^{Q(0)/2}\dfrac{\left[\pi\sigma_{n}(0)\right]^{\rho/2}}{\beta_{n}^{1/2}},

(S4.18)

\displaystyle p_{n-1}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle\approx-\cos\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{2}\right)\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2b_{n}}\dfrac{2^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}}}{\Gamma\left[\frac{3+\rho}{2}\right]}e^{Q(0)/2}\dfrac{\left[\pi\sigma_{n}(0)\right]^{1+\rho/2}}{\beta_{n}^{1/2}}\left(1+\dfrac{\rho^{2}-1}{2\pi\beta_{n}\sigma_{n}(0)}\right),

(S4.19)

For the CD kernel, the Christoffel-Darboux formula
K_{n}(0,0)=b_{n}\left(p_{n}^{\prime}(0)p_{n-1}(0)-p_{n-1}^{\prime}(0)p_{n}(0)\right)
gives

K_{n}(0,0)\approx\dfrac{1}{2^{1+\rho}\Gamma\left[\frac{1}{2}(1+\rho)\right]\Gamma\left[\frac{1}{2}(3+\rho)\right]}e^{Q(0)}\left[\pi\sigma_{n}(0)\right]^{1+\rho}.

(S4.20)

This proves
Eq.

83
of the main text, with a multiplicative error term of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))
coming from neglecting the
R(0)-\mathds{1}
and
R^{\prime}(0)
contributions.

S4.2
Expressions for the spectral bootstrap

S4.2.1
Bessel bootstrap: behavior near the origin

First let us derive
Eq.

106
of the main text. Squaring
Eqs.

S4.14
and
S4.17
and using the expressions
a_{+}(x)=e^{i\pi/4}(1-x)^{1/4}/(1+x)^{1/4}
and
\varphi_{+}(x)=e^{i\arccos(x)}
, we get (after some algebraic simplifications)

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(\beta_{n}x)^{2}

\displaystyle+p_{n}(\beta_{n}x)^{2}\approx\dfrac{nf_{n}(x)}{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\times\Bigg[\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}+J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}\right)(nf_{n}(x))

(S4.21)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{n}x\left(\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}-J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}\right)(nf_{n}(x))\sin[\rho\arcsin{x}]+2\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}\right)(nf_{n}(x))\cos[\rho\arcsin{x}]\right)\Bigg].

Then we use the fact that
nf_{n}(x)=\pi I_{n}(\beta_{n}x)
with
I_{n}
defined in
Eq.

97
. Rescaling
\beta_{n}x\mapsto\omega
, and using
w(x)=x^{\rho}\exp[-Q(x)]
, we get

\displaystyle e^{-Q(\omega)}

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{p_{n-1}(\omega)^{2}+p_{n}(\omega)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}^{2}-\omega^{2}}}\dfrac{\pi I_{n}(\omega)}{\omega^{\rho}}\Bigg[\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}+J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))

(S4.22)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle(-1)^{n}\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\left(\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}-J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))\sin\left\{\rho\arcsin\left(\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)\right\}+2\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))\cos\left\{\rho\arcsin\left(\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)\right\}\right)\Bigg].

This simplifies to
Eq.

106
upon dropping the term proportional to
\sin\{\rho\arcsin(\omega/\beta_{n})\}
, which for
\omega/\beta_{n}\ll 1
is subleading by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}\sigma_{n}(\omega))
relative to the term proportional to
\cos\{\rho\arcsin(\omega/\beta_{n})\}
.

Now let us derive the expression
Eq.

107
involving the diagonal Christoffel-Darboux kernel. The general strategy will be to use the Christoffel-Darboux formula
K_{n}(\omega,\omega)=b_{n}\left(p_{n}^{\prime}(\omega)p_{n-1}(\omega)-p_{n-1}^{\prime}(\omega)p_{n}(\omega)\right)
, together with the expressions
Eqs.

S4.14
and
S4.17
we derived for
p_{n}
and
p_{n-1}
. After a lengthy but straightforward algebraic computation, we get the expression

\displaystyle\hskip-30.00005ptK_{n}(\omega,\omega)

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{4}\dfrac{\pi I_{n}(\omega)}{w(\omega)}\Bigg(\pi\sigma_{n}(\omega)\left[J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}+J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}-J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-3)}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}-J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+3)}\right](\pi I_{n}(\omega))+\dfrac{\rho\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}+J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}^{2}-\omega^{2}}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(-1)^{n}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{\beta_{n}^{2}-\omega^{2}}\left[\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{2}-J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{2}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))\cos\left\{\rho\arcsin\left(\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)\right\}+2\left(J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}J_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}\right)(\pi I_{n}(\omega))\sin\left\{\rho\arcsin\left(\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)\right\}\right]\Bigg),

(S4.23)

where we used
\partial_{\omega}I_{n}(\omega)=\sigma_{n}(\omega)
. This expression reduces to
Eq.

107
after dropping all but the first term proportional to
\pi\sigma_{n}(\omega)
in the large curly bracketed expression, since for
\omega/\beta_{n}\ll 1
the other terms are subleading by a factor of either
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}\sigma_{n}(\omega))
or
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}^{2}/\omega^{2})
.

S4.2.2
Bulk bootstrap

Having already derived the spectral bootstrap equations near an algebraic divergence
\Phi(\omega)\sim|\omega|^{\rho}
, a quick way to arrive at the spectral bootstrap equations in the bulk is to simply send
\rho\to 0
and then replace
\exp[-Q(\omega)]\mapsto\Phi(\omega)/2\pi
. One can check that properly doing the calculation by unpacking the expression for
Y(z)
in the bulk gives the same expressions.

To derive
Eq.

100
of the main text, we send
\rho\to 0
in
Eq.

S4.22
; using
J_{\frac{-1}{2}}(x)=\sqrt{2/\pi x}\cos{x}
and
J_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)=\sqrt{2/\pi x}\sin{x}
, we get

e^{-Q(\omega)}\approx\dfrac{1}{p_{n-1}(\omega)^{2}+p_{n}(\omega)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}^{2}-\omega^{2}}}\dfrac{2}{\pi}\left[1-(-1)^{n}\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\sin[2\pi I_{n}(\omega)]\right],

(S4.24)

which is equivalent to
Eq.

100
once we set
\Phi(\omega)\equiv 2\pi\exp[-Q(\omega)]
.

To get
Eq.

102
of the main text, we take
\rho\to 0
of
Eq.

S4.23
to get

K_{n}(\omega,\omega)\approx\dfrac{2\pi\sigma_{n}(\omega)}{\Phi(\omega)}\left(1-(-1)^{n}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{\beta_{n}^{2}-\omega^{2}}\dfrac{\cos[2\pi I_{n}(\omega)]}{2\pi\sigma_{n}(\omega)}\right).

(S4.25)

This reduces to
Eq.

102
upon dropping the second term in the brackets, which is subleading by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}\sigma_{n}(\omega))
.
From
Eqs.

S2.127
and
S2.129
, this asymptotic has a multiplicative error of
\mathcal{O}(|\omega|^{-1}\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))+\mathcal{O}(1/n)
, where the factor of
|\omega|^{-1}
gets cut off by
\mathcal{O}(1)
for
|\omega|\leq\mathcal{O}(1)
.

S4.2.3
Airy bootstrap: behavior near the edge

In this section we will consider frequencies near the edge of the spectrum,
\omega\approx\beta_{n}
. Similarly to
Section

S4.1
, we reverse the transformations
Y\mapsto U\mapsto T\mapsto S\mapsto R
, but now within the local parametrix centered at
z=1
. With the aim of approaching the real axis, we will take
z
in the sector mapped to sector II of
Fig.

S2
. Combining
Eqs.

S2.7
,
S2.34
,
S2.45
,
S2.57
,
S2.117
and
S2.118
, this gives

\displaystyle Y(\beta_{n}z)

\displaystyle=\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(z)E(z)\Psi(f_{n}(z))e^{-n\phi_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}z^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}

(S4.26)

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
z^{-\rho}e^{-2n\phi_{n}(z)}&1\end{pmatrix}e^{-(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{ng_{n}(z)\sigma_{3}}\beta_{n}^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}.

The first column of
Y
is then given by

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{21}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=(\beta_{n}z)^{-\rho/2}e^{n\left[g_{n}(z)-\phi_{n}(z)-(l_{n}/2)\right]}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(z)E(z)\Psi(f_{n}(z))\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.27)

Taking
z\downarrow x<1
, using
Eq.

S4.10
, and replacing
\Psi
with the sector II expression from
Eq.

S2.61
, we get

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(\beta_{n}x)\\
Y_{21}(\beta_{n}x)\end{pmatrix}=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{-i\pi/4}(\beta_{n}x)^{-\rho/2}e^{\frac{1}{2}Q(\beta_{n}x)}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(x)E(x)\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(f_{n}(x))\\
\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))\end{pmatrix},

(S4.28)

where we recall that both
E(z)
and
R(z)
are analytic near
z=1
, and the biholomorphic map
f_{n}(z)
is defined in
Proposition

S2
. Using the definition
Eq.

S2.72
of
E(x)
, after some algebra this becomes

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Y_{11}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{21}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=\sqrt{\pi}e^{-i\pi/4}(\beta_{n}x)^{-\rho/2}e^{\frac{1}{2}Q(\beta_{n}x)}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(nl_{n}/2)\sigma_{3}}R(x)2^{-(\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{(i\pi/4)\sigma_{3}}

(S4.29)

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}\left[ia_{+}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)+a_{+}^{-1}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{1/4}\Ai(f_{n})+\left[-a_{+}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)-ia_{+}^{-1}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n})\\[10.00002pt]
\left[-ia_{+}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)+a_{+}^{-1}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{1/4}\Ai(f_{n})+\left[a_{+}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)-ia_{+}^{-1}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n})\end{pmatrix},

where for notational simplicity we have suppressed the arguments of
f_{n}\equiv f_{n}(x)
and
a_{+}\equiv a_{+}(x)=e^{i\pi/4}(1-x)^{1/4}/(1+x)^{1/4}
, and used
\varphi_{+}(x)=e^{i\arccos{x}}
for
0<x<1
. Approximating
R(x)\approx\mathds{1}
, then using
Eqs.

S4.1
and
S4.2
to convert to
p_{n}(z)
and
p_{n-1}(z)
, and the asymptotic
Eq.

S3.29
for the leading coefficient
y_{n}
, we get

\displaystyle\textstyle\hskip-41.62473ptp_{n}(\beta_{n}x)\approx\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)}}\left(\left[ia_{+}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)+a_{+}^{-1}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{1/4}\Ai(f_{n})+\left[-a_{+}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)-ia_{+}^{-1}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n})\right),

(S4.30)

\displaystyle\textstyle\hskip-50.87466ptp_{n-1}(\beta_{n}x)\approx\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{2b_{n}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)}}\left(\left[-ia_{+}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)+a_{+}^{-1}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{1/4}\Ai(f_{n})+\left[a_{+}\cos\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)-ia_{+}^{-1}\sin\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\arccos{x}\right)\right]f_{n}^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n})\right).

(S4.31)

Note that, by
Theorem

1
, the prefactor
\beta_{n}/2b_{n}=1+\mathcal{O}(1/n)
, so we will henceforth approximate it as 1. Now, from the definition of
f_{n}(z)
in
Proposition

S2
, we can deduce that the behavior of
f_{n}(x)
near
x=1
is approximately

f_{n}(x)\approx(x-1)f_{n}^{\prime}(1),\quad x\to 1,

(S4.32)

where the derivative
f_{n}^{\prime}(1)
is related to the equilibrium measure by

f_{n}^{\prime}(1)=\left(n\hat{\varphi}_{n}(1)\right)^{2/3}=\left(\dfrac{nh_{n}(1)}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2/3}.

(S4.33)

This allows us to take the limit
x\to 1^{-}
of
Eqs.

S4.30
and
S4.31
, with the result

\displaystyle p_{n}(\beta_{n})

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n})}}\left[(2nh_{n}(1))^{1/6}\Ai(0)-(\rho+1)(2nh_{n}(1))^{-1/6}\Ai^{\prime}(0)\right],

(S4.34)

\displaystyle p_{n-1}(\beta_{n})

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n})}}\left[(2nh_{n}(1))^{1/6}\Ai(0)-(\rho-1)(2nh_{n}(1))^{-1/6}\Ai^{\prime}(0)\right],

(S4.35)

where
\Ai(0)=(3^{\frac{2}{3}}\Gamma[\frac{2}{3}])^{-1}
and
\Ai^{\prime}(0)=-(3^{\frac{1}{3}}\Gamma[\frac{1}{3}])^{-1}
. By taking the ratio of these expressions we eliminate the unknown weight
w(\beta_{n})
and get a quadratic equation in
(2nh_{n}(1))^{1/6}
, either solution of which gives

h_{n}(1)\approx\dfrac{1}{2n}\left(\dfrac{\Ai(0)}{\Ai^{\prime}(0)}\right)^{3}\left[\rho-\left(\dfrac{p_{n}(\beta_{n})+p_{n-1}(\beta_{n})}{p_{n}(\beta_{n})-p_{n-1}(\beta_{n})}\right)\right]^{3}.

(S4.36)

This gives a means of determining
h_{n}(1)
solely in terms of the orthogonal polynomials, which can be computed in terms of the Lanczos coefficients using the three-term recurrence. One can then determine the weight
w(\beta_{n})
at the endpoint
\omega=\beta_{n}
by inverting
Eq.

S4.34
:

w(\beta_{n})\approx\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}p_{n}(\beta_{n})^{2}}\left[(2nh_{n}(1))^{1/6}\Ai(0)-(\rho+1)(2nh_{n}(1))^{-1/6}\Ai^{\prime}(0)\right].

(S4.37)

To get the first of the bootstrap equations, we sum the squares of
Eqs.

S4.30
and
S4.31
and rearrange for
w(\beta_{n}x)
to give

\displaystyle w(\beta_{n}x)

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{2}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{1}{p_{n}(\beta_{n}x)^{2}+p_{n-1}(\beta_{n}x)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\Big[-2x\Ai\big(f_{n}(x)\big)\Ai^{\prime}\big(f_{n}(x)\big)\sin\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]

(S4.38)

\displaystyle+\big(-f_{n}(x)\big)^{1/2}\Ai\big(f_{n}(x)\big)^{2}\big(x\cos\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]+1\big)-\big(-f_{n}(x)\big)^{-1/2}\Ai^{\prime}\big(f_{n}(x)\big)^{2}\big(x\cos\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]-1\big)\Big].

To get the second bootstrap equation concerning the derivative
f_{n}^{\prime}(x)
, we need to compute the Christoffel-Darboux kernel
K_{n}(\beta_{n}x,\beta_{n}x)
, which involves differentiating the expressions
Eqs.

S4.30
and
S4.31
for
p_{n}(\beta_{n}x)
and
p_{n-1}(\beta_{n}x)
. Note that, because of the determinantal structure of
K_{n}
(c.f.
Eq.

S4.6
), it is not necessary to differentiate the weight
w(\beta_{n}x)
, since all terms involving its derivative cancel exactly. It is then a matter of algebra to arrive at the expression

\displaystyle f_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{2\Ai^{2}f_{n}^{2}-2(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}f_{n}-\Ai\Ai^{\prime}}\Big[-2\beta_{n}w(\beta_{n}x)K_{n}(\beta_{n}x,\beta_{n}x)f_{n}+\frac{2}{1-x^{2}}\Ai\Ai^{\prime}\cos[\rho\arccos{x}]f_{n}

(S4.39)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\sin[\rho\arccos{x}]}{1-x^{2}}\left(\Ai^{2}\big(-f_{n}\big)^{3/2}-(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}(-f_{n})^{1/2}\right)-\dfrac{\rho}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\left(\Ai^{2}\big(-f_{n}\big)^{3/2}+(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}(-f_{n})^{1/2}\right)\Big],

where we have suppressed the arguments of
f_{n}\equiv f_{n}(x)
,
\Ai\equiv\Ai(f_{n}(x))
, and
\Ai^{\prime}\equiv\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))
. From
Eqs.

S2.127
and
S2.129
, this expression has a multiplicative error of
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
from neglecting the contributions from
R(z)-\mathds{1}
and
R^{\prime}(z)
.

S4.3
Level-
n
Green’s function

In this section we derive the expressions in
Eqs.

67
and
64
for the level-
n
Green’s function
G_{n}(z)
. From the definition in
Eq.

61
, we can deduce that

G_{n}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu^{(n)}(x)}{z-x},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

(S4.40)

where
\mu^{(n)}
is the spectral measure which generates the ‘associated orthogonal polynomials’
\{p_{m}^{(n)}\}_{m=0}^{\infty}
, which are defined by the ‘
n
-shifted’ recursion relation

b_{m+1+n}p_{m+1}^{(n)}(x)=xp_{m}^{(n)}(x)-b_{m+n}p_{m-1}^{(n)}(x),\quad m\geq 0,

(S4.41)

with initial conditions
p_{-1}^{(n)}(x)=0
,
p_{0}^{(n)}(x)=1
. For
n=0
we have
\mathrm{d}\mu^{(0)}(x)=w(x)\mathrm{d}x
, where
w
is the original weight function.

The starting point for our analysis will be the relation

G_{n}(z)=\dfrac{1}{b_{n}}\dfrac{C_{n}(z)}{C_{n-1}(z)},

(S4.42)

where

C_{n}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{p_{n}(x)}{z-x}w(x)\mathrm{d}x,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},

(S4.43)

is the weighted Cauchy-Stieltjes transform of
p_{n}(x)
with respect to the original weight function
w
. This relation appears as Eq. (3.7) in Ref.
[
59
]
. This reformulation is helpful because these weighted Cauchy-Stieltjes transforms appear (up to a normalization factor) in the second column of the solution
Y(z)
to the fundamental Riemann-Hilbert problem discussed in
Section

S2.1
. Indeed, a short computation gives

G_{n}(z)=\dfrac{1}{(Y_{1})_{12}}\dfrac{Y_{12}(z)}{Y_{22}(z)},

(S4.44)

where we used the fundamental solution
Eq.

S2.4
, together with the relations
b_{n}=y_{n-1}/y_{n}
and
Eqs.

S2.5
and
S2.6
. We have already determined the asymptotics of
(Y_{1})_{12}
in
Eq.

S3.28
, so it remains to determine the scaling of
Y_{12}(z)
and
Y_{22}(z)
.

S4.3.1
Behavior away from
z=0
and
z=\pm\beta_{n}

Now let us derive the expression
Eq.

64
for the level-
n
Green’s function away from the special points
z=0
and
z=\pm\beta_{n}
. Unpacking the transformations
Eqs.

S2.7
,
S2.34
,
S2.45
and
S2.118
for
Y(z)
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{12}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{22}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=\beta_{n}^{\rho/2}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}}e^{-ng_{n}(z)}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}R(z)N(z)\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.45)

Note that, since we have focused only on the second column of
Y(z)
, this expression is accurate regardless of whether
z
is inside or outside the lens, provided it is not near the centers
z=0
and
z=\pm\beta_{n}
of the local parametrices constructed in
Sections

S2.9
and
S2.10
. Approximating
R(z)\approx\mathds{1}
, and using the form
Eq.

S2.53
for the outside solution
N(z)
, we get

\dfrac{Y_{12}(\beta_{n}z)}{Y_{22}(\beta_{n}z)}\approx-i2^{-\rho}\beta_{n}^{2n+\rho}e^{nl_{n}}\left(\dfrac{a(z)-a(z)^{-1}}{a(z)+a(z)^{-1}}\right).

(S4.46)

Using the definition
a(z)=(z-1)^{1/4}/(z+1)^{1/4}
and the asymptotic form
Eq.

S3.28
for
(Y_{1})_{12}
, we get
Eq.

64
of the main text:

\beta_{n}G_{n}(\beta_{n}z)\approx 2\left(z-\sqrt{z+1}\sqrt{z-1}\right).

(S4.47)

There will be a multiplicative error from the
R(z)\approx\mathds{1}
approximation; by the discussion surrounding
Eq.

S2.127
, this error will be
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
for
z=\mathcal{O}(1)
. For
\rho=0
it will remain of this order even as
z
shrinks to 0, but for
\rho\neq 0
the error will grow like
\mathcal{O}(1/(nh_{n}(0)|z|))
, eventually reaching
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))
at
z\sim 1/\beta_{n}
.

S4.3.2
Behavior near the origin

We will focus on the limiting behavior as
z
approaches the positive real axis from
\mathbb{C}_{+}
; the other limits can be computed analogously. Starting from
Eq.

S4.7
, for
z
in the first quadrant and satisfying
|\beta_{n}z|\ll 1
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{12}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{22}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=(\beta_{n}z)^{\rho/2}e^{-\frac{i\pi\rho}{2}}e^{-n[g_{n}(z)-\phi_{n}(z)-l_{n}/2]}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}R(z)E_{n}(z)\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.48)

Using the expression
Eq.

S2.96
for
\Psi_{\rho/2}(nf_{n}(z))
with
z
in the first quadrant, and taking the limit
z\downarrow x\in(0,\delta_{n})
, this becomes

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{12,+}(\beta_{n}x)\\
Y_{22,+}(\beta_{n}x)\end{pmatrix}=\dfrac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\sqrt{w(\beta_{n}x)}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}\sqrt{nf_{n}(x)}R(x)E_{n}(x)\begin{pmatrix}H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{(1)}(nf_{n}(x))\\[5.0pt]
H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{(1)}(nf_{n}(x))\end{pmatrix}.

(S4.49)

Using
Eq.

S2.110
for
E_{n}(x)
, approximating
R(x)\approx\mathds{1}
, and using the asymptotic
Eq.

S3.28
for
(Y_{1})_{12}
, we get

G_{n,+}(\beta_{n}x)\approx-\dfrac{2}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{\begin{pmatrix}&H^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}(nf_{n}(x))\left[e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho-1)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho-1)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\right]\\[5.0pt]
+&H^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}(nf_{n}(x))\left[e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho+1)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho+1)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\right]\end{pmatrix}}{\begin{pmatrix}&H^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}(nf_{n}(x))\left[e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho-1)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho-1)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\right]\\[5.0pt]
+&H^{(1)}_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}(nf_{n}(x))\left[e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho+1)}\varphi_{+}^{-\rho/2}(a_{+}+a_{+}^{-1})+e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(2n-\rho+1)}\varphi_{+}^{\rho/2}(a_{+}-a_{+}^{-1})\right]\end{pmatrix}},

(S4.50)

where we have suppressed the arguments of
\varphi_{+}\equiv\varphi_{+}(x)
and
a_{+}\equiv a_{+}(x)
. Let us now rescale
\beta_{n}x\mapsto\omega
. As
\omega\to 0
, both
\varphi_{+}(\omega/\beta_{n})
and
a_{+}(\omega/\beta_{n})
tend to
\mathcal{O}(1)
constants, while in general the Hankel functions will have an algebraic divergence. Thus, to leading order in
\omega/\beta_{n}\ll 1
, we can approximate
\varphi_{+}(\omega/\beta_{n})\approx\varphi_{+}(0)=i
and
a_{+}(\omega/\beta_{n})\approx a_{+}(0)=e^{i\pi/4}
. With these approximations, we get the simpler expression

G_{n}(\omega+i0^{+})\approx-\dfrac{2}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{\cos\left(\frac{n\pi}{2}\right)H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{(1)}(\pi I_{n}(\omega))+\sin\left(\frac{n\pi}{2}\right)H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{(1)}(\pi I_{n}(\omega))}{\cos\left(\frac{n\pi}{2}\right)H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho+1)}^{(1)}(\pi I_{n}(\omega))-\sin\left(\frac{n\pi}{2}\right)H_{\frac{1}{2}(\rho-1)}^{(1)}(\pi I_{n}(\omega))},

(S4.51)

where we have replaced
nf_{n}(\omega/\beta_{n})=\pi I_{n}(\omega)
. Note that the difference between even and odd
n
is now manifest: for
-1<\rho<1
we have
G_{2n}(\omega+i0^{+})\sim\omega^{\rho}
as
\omega\to 0
while
G_{2n+1}(\omega+i0^{+})\sim 1/\omega^{\rho}
, consistent with the recursion relation
Eq.

62
. Taking
n
even and approximating
I_{n}(\omega)=\int_{0}^{\omega}\sigma_{n}(\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}\approx\sigma_{n}(0)\omega
, we recover
Eq.

67
of the main text. By the discussion surrounding
Eq.

S2.126
, this expression has a multiplicative error of
\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}/nh_{n}(0))
.

S4.3.3
Behavior near
z=\pm\beta_{n}

Finally let us derive an expression for the level-
n
Green’s function near the endpoints
z=\pm\beta_{n}
. We will focus on the behavior near
z=\beta_{n}
, with similar results holding near
z=-\beta_{n}
. Starting from
Eq.

S4.26
, the second column of
Y
in a neighborhood of
\beta_{n}
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{12}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{22}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=(\beta_{n}z)^{\rho/2}e^{-n[g_{n}(z)-\phi_{n}(z)-l_{n}/2]}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}R(z)E(z)\Psi(f_{n}(z))\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},

(S4.52)

where the Airy parametrix
\Psi(\zeta)
was defined in
Eq.

S2.61
, and the map
f_{n}(z)
was defined in
Eq.

S2.68
. Focusing on
z
in quadrant
I
of
Fig.

S2
, this becomes

\begin{pmatrix}Y_{12}(\beta_{n}z)\\
Y_{22}(\beta_{n}z)\end{pmatrix}=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\frac{i\pi}{12}}(\beta_{n}z)^{\rho/2}e^{-n[g_{n}(z)-\phi_{n}(z)-l_{n}/2]}\beta_{n}^{(n+\rho/2)\sigma_{3}}e^{\frac{nl_{n}}{2}\sigma_{3}}R(z)E(z)\begin{pmatrix}\Ai(\xi_{n}(z))\\
e^{\frac{4\pi i}{3}}\Ai^{\prime}(\xi_{n}(z))\end{pmatrix},

(S4.53)

where
\xi_{n}(z)\equiv e^{4\pi i/3}f_{n}(z)
. Using the expression
Eq.

S2.72
for
E(z)
, approximating
R(z)\approx\mathds{1}
, and using the asymptotic formula
Eq.

S3.28
, substituting into
Eq.

S4.44
gives

G_{n}(\beta_{n}z)\approx\dfrac{-2}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{\begin{pmatrix}&\left[a(z)(\varphi^{\rho/2}-\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)+a(z)^{-1}(\varphi^{\rho/2}+\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)\right]\xi_{n}(z)^{1/4}\Ai(\xi_{n}(z))\\
-&\left[a(z)(\varphi^{\rho/2}+\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)+a(z)^{-1}(\varphi^{\rho/2}-\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)\right]\xi_{n}(z)^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(\xi_{n}(z))\end{pmatrix}}{\begin{pmatrix}&\left[a(z)(\varphi^{\rho/2}-\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)-a(z)^{-1}(\varphi^{\rho/2}+\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)\right]\xi_{n}(z)^{1/4}\Ai(\xi_{n}(z))\\
-&\left[a(z)(\varphi^{\rho/2}+\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)-a(z)^{-1}(\varphi^{\rho/2}-\varphi^{-\rho/2})(z)\right]\xi_{n}(z)^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(\xi_{n}(z))\end{pmatrix}},

(S4.54)

where
a(z)=(z-1)^{1/4}/(z+1)^{1/4}
and
\varphi(z)=z+(z-1)^{1/2}(z+1)^{1/2}
. Near
z=1
, we have
f_{n}(z)\approx f_{n}^{\prime}(1)(z-1)
, where
f_{n}^{\prime}(1)=(nh_{n}(1)/\sqrt{2})^{2/3}\sim\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})
. Then for
z=1+\mathcal{O}(n^{-2/3})
, we have
G_{n}(\beta_{n}z)\approx G_{n}(\beta_{n})+\mathcal{O}(z-1)
, with

G_{n}(\beta_{n})\approx\dfrac{2}{\beta_{n}}\left(\dfrac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(1)^{1/2}\Gamma\left[\frac{1}{3}\right]-\sqrt{2}(-3)^{1/3}(\rho+1)\Gamma\left[\frac{2}{3}\right]}{2f_{n}^{\prime}(1)^{1/2}\Gamma\left[\frac{1}{3}\right]-\sqrt{2}(-3)^{1/3}(\rho-1)\Gamma\left[\frac{2}{3}\right]}\right).

(S4.55)

Since
f_{n}^{\prime}(1)\sim\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, we conclude that
G_{n}(\beta_{n})\approx 2/\beta_{n}
to leading order as
n\to\infty
. By the discussion surrounding
Eq.

S2.127
, this expression has a multiplicative error of
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
.

S5
Detailed analysis of the equilibrium measure

S5.1
Behavior of the equilibrium measure near the origin

S5.1.1
Behavior at the origin

Lemma S8
.

Consider
W=\exp(-Q(x))
with
Q\in\mathrm{VSLF}(p,q)
for
p\geq 1
.

If
p>1
, then for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}(0)=\dfrac{2p}{p-1}.

(S5.1)

If
p=1
and
q>-1
, then as
n\to\infty
we have

h_{n}(0)=(\log{n})^{1+o(1)}.

(S5.2)

Proof.

(Case:
p>1
)

From
Eq.

S2.13
we have

h_{n}(0)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{s}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}.

(S5.3)

From
Lemma

S1
(v) we know that
\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}^{\prime}(s)=(p/\lambda_{p})s^{p-1}
, uniformly for
s
in any compact subinterval of
(0,\infty)
, where the constant
\lambda_{p}
is defined in
Eq.

S1.11
.
This implies pointwise convergence of the integrand for
s\in(0,1)
. In order to apply the dominated convergence theorem we appeal to
Eq.

S1.32

choosing
\epsilon
so that
p-1-\epsilon>0
. However, this upper bound is only available for
s\in[A/\beta_{n},1]
. Observe that the remaining part of the integral can be bounded by

\int_{0}^{A/\beta_{n}}\dfrac{|V_{n}^{\prime}(s)|}{s}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\leq\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-(A/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{A}\dfrac{|Q^{\prime}(u)|}{u}\mathrm{d}u\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\right),

(S5.4)

because
Q^{\prime}(0)=0
due to the assumed evenness of
Q
. As
\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n}/n=0
for
p>1
by
Eq.

S1.13
we have shown

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}(0)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{p}{\lambda_{p}}\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{s^{p-2}}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\mathrm{d}s,

(S5.5)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2p}{p-1},

(S5.6)

where the last step follows from evaluating the integral and using the definition of
\lambda_{p}
.

(Case:
p=1
)

The proof for
p>1
does not work for
p=1
because the limiting integrand is no longer integrable, having a logarithmic divergence at the origin. We will therefore need to be more careful about handling this divergence. From
Eq.

S2.13
, we have the integral expression for
h_{n}(0)
,

\displaystyle h_{n}(0)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{Q^{\prime}(u)}{u\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\mathrm{d}u.

(S5.7)

where we have changed variables for convenience. We split the integral into three parts,
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
, and
I_{3}
,

I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\equiv\left(\int_{0}^{A_{n}}+\int_{A_{n}}^{\beta_{n}/2}+\int_{\beta_{n}/2}^{\beta_{n}}\right)\dfrac{Q^{\prime}(u)}{u\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\mathrm{d}u,

(S5.8)

where
we define
A_{n}
as

A_{n}\coloneqq(\log{n})^{\alpha},\quad\text{for some }\alpha>0.

(S5.9)

We show that the integrals
I_{3}
and
I_{1}
are dominated by
I_{2}
. Estimating

1\leq\left[1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}\right]^{-1/2}\leq 2/\sqrt{3},\quad\text{for all }A_{n}\leq u\leq\beta_{n}/2,

(S5.10)

and using
Lemma

S1
(iii) we obtain for
I_{2}
lower and upper bounds

\Omega\left((\log{\beta_{n})^{q+1-\epsilon/2}}\right)\leq I_{2}\leq\mathcal{O}\left((\log{\beta_{n})^{q+1+\epsilon/2}}\right),

(S5.11)

where we have chosen
\epsilon
so small that
q+1-\epsilon/2>0
. The dominance of
I_{2}
now follows from

|I_{3}|\leq 2(\log{\beta_{n}})^{q+\epsilon/2}\int_{\beta_{n}/2}^{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{O}\left((\log{\beta_{n}})^{q+\epsilon/2}\right),

(S5.12)

|I_{1}|=\mathcal{O}\left((\log{A_{n})^{q+1+\epsilon/2}}\right),

(S5.13)

where the latter estimate uses again
Lemma

S1
(iii) and that
Q^{\prime}(u)/u
is bounded on any compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
due to
Q^{\prime}(0)=0
. Note further that
\beta_{n}/n=\Theta\left(1/Q^{\prime}(\beta_{n})\right)
; see
Lemma

S1
(iv). Then it follows from
Eqs.

S5.7
and
S5.11
and
Lemma

S1
(iii)
that

\Omega\left((\log{\beta_{n})^{1-\epsilon}}\right)\leq h_{n}(0)\leq\mathcal{O}\left((\log{\beta_{n})^{1+\epsilon}}\right),

(S5.14)

and the claim follows from
Lemma

S1
(vi) as
\epsilon
can be chosen arbitrarily small.
∎

S5.1.2
Uniform lower bounds near the origin

In this section we derive uniform lower bounds on the real part of
h_{n}(z)
for
z
near the origin. The general strategy will be to reduce to the analysis we performed for
z=0
.

Lemma S9
.

Consider
Q\in\mathrm{CVSLF}(p,q,\theta,\gamma)
. Let
0<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
,
0<\theta^{\prime}<\theta
, and
\alpha^{\prime}\geq 0
arbitrary for
p>1
but constrained to
\alpha^{\prime}<1+q
for
p=1
. Define the set
\Lambda_{n}
by

\Lambda_{n}\coloneqq\{\lambda\in C_{\theta^{\prime}}:|\lambda|<(\log{n})^{\alpha^{\prime}}\}\,\cup\,\left\{\lambda:|\lambda|<\gamma^{\prime}\right\},

(S5.15)

Then, as
n\to\infty
, we have uniformly for
z\in\Lambda_{n}/\beta_{n}
that

h_{n}(z)=h_{n}(0)\left[1+o(1)\right].

(S5.16)

Together with the scaling of
h_{n}(0)
derived in
Eq.

S5.2
, we conclude that, for sufficiently large
n
, one can obtain a uniform lower bound, say
h_{n}(0)/2
, for the real part of
h_{n}(z)
if
z\in\Lambda_{n}/\beta_{n}
.

Proof.

Choose
\alpha>\alpha^{\prime}
arbitrary for
p>1
but still constrained to
\alpha<1+q
for
p=1
. Set
A_{n}:=\left(\log{n}\right)^{\alpha}
and denote
r(z)=(z+1)^{1/2}(z-1)^{1/2}
. By the discussion in
Section

S2.4
, we may represent
h_{n}(z)
as

h_{n}(z)=-2\left[\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\left(\int_{-1}^{-A_{n}/\beta_{n}}+\int_{A_{n}/\beta_{n}}^{1}\right)\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{r_{+}(s)(s-z)}\mathrm{d}s+\dfrac{1/2}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma_{n}/\beta_{n}}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{r(s)(s-z)}\mathrm{d}s\right],

(S5.17)

where
r_{+}(s)=i\sqrt{1-s^{2}}
, and
\Gamma_{n}
are simply closed clockwise oriented curves as sketched in
Fig.

S5
. Important features of the contours are the following. i)
\Gamma_{n}
is contained in
C_{\theta}\cup\left\{z:|z|<\gamma\right\}
and has a positive
n
-independent lower bound on its distance from the set
\Lambda_{n}
(see (
S5.15
)) that is contained in the interior of

\Gamma_{n}
. ii) Moreover, the rightmost and leftmost points of
\Gamma_{n}
are
A_{n}
and
-A_{n}
respectively. iii) The length of
\Gamma_{n}
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(A_{n})
.

\Gamma_{n}
A_{n}=(\log n)^{\alpha}
\gamma
\gamma^{\prime}
(\log n)^{\alpha^{\prime}}
\Lambda_{n}
-A_{n}

Figure S5:
Sketch of the contour
\Gamma_{n}
, and the sets
\Lambda_{n}
and
C_{\theta^{\prime}}\cup\left\{z:|z|<\gamma\right\}
.

First we employ the symmetries of
Q
and
r
and change variables in the integrals to
u=\beta_{n}s
. Denoting
\lambda\equiv\beta_{n}z
we obtain

h_{n}(z)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\left[\int_{A_{n}}^{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{uQ^{\prime}(u)}{(u^{2}-\lambda^{2})\sqrt{1-(u/{\beta_{n})^{2}}}}\mathrm{d}u+\dfrac{i}{4}\oint_{\Gamma_{n}}\dfrac{Q^{\prime}(u)}{r(u/\beta_{n})(u-\lambda)}\mathrm{d}u\right].

(S5.18)

In order to reduce to the
\lambda=0
case, we want to examine the effect of replacing
1/(u^{2}-\lambda^{2})\to 1/u^{2}
. To that end, we consider the difference

\dfrac{1}{u^{2}-\lambda^{2}}-\dfrac{1}{u^{2}}=\dfrac{\lambda^{2}}{u^{2}(u^{2}-\lambda^{2})},

(S5.19)

so that

\dfrac{\pi}{2}\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}h_{n}(z)=\int_{A_{n}}^{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{Q^{\prime}(u)}{u\sqrt{1-(u/{\beta_{n})^{2}}}}\left(1+\dfrac{\lambda^{2}}{u^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}u+\dfrac{i}{4}\oint_{\Gamma_{n}}\dfrac{Q^{\prime}(u)}{r(u/\beta_{n})(u-\lambda)}\mathrm{d}u\equiv J_{1}+J_{2}.

(S5.20)

Recall from
Eqs.

S5.7
and
S5.8
that

\dfrac{\pi}{2}\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}h_{n}(0)=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3},

(S5.21)

a representation that also holds in the case
p>1
. We therefore need to establish
(J_{1}+J_{2})/(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})=1+o(1)
: We prove this by verifying that the following limits for
n\to\infty
hold uniformly in
z
:

\dfrac{I_{1}}{I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}}\to 0,\quad\dfrac{J_{1}}{I_{2}+I_{3}}\to 1,\quad\dfrac{J_{2}}{I_{2}+I_{3}}\to 0.

(S5.22)

The second claim follows from the observation that for
(\log{n})^{\alpha}=A_{n}\leq u\leq\beta_{n}
and
|\lambda|\leq(\log{n})^{\alpha^{\prime}}
one has

\dfrac{\lambda^{2}}{u^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left((\log{n})^{-2(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})}\right).

(S5.23)

The first claim has already been established in the case
p=1
, see
Eqs.

S5.11
,
S5.12
and
S5.13
. In the case
p>1
, it follows from
Lemma

S1
(iii) and from the boundedness of
Q^{\prime}(u)/u
on bounded sets that
I_{1}=\mathcal{O}\left((\log{n})^{\delta_{1}}\right)
, whereas by
Lemma

S1
(vi) and
Eq.

S5.2
we have

I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}=\Omega\left(n/\beta_{n}\right)=\Omega\left(n^{\delta_{2}}\right)
, for some positive numbers
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
. Consequently
I_{1}/(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Finally, we turn to the third claim. Observe that by the choice of the path of integration
\Gamma_{n}
the denominator of the integrand of the term
J_{2}
is uniformly bounded away from
0
.
Using
Eq.

S1.21
we obtain the sup-norm bound
\mathcal{O}\left(A_{n}^{p-1}(\log{A_{n}})^{q+\epsilon/2}\right)
on the integrand yielding

J_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(A_{n}^{p}(\log{A_{n}})^{q+\epsilon/2}\right)
. For
p>1
we have
I_{2}+I_{3}=\Omega(n/\beta_{n})
which establishes
J_{2}/(I_{2}+I_{3})\to 0
. In the case
p=1

we learn from
Eqs.

S5.11
and
S5.12
that
I_{2}+I_{3}=\Omega\left((\log{\beta_{n})^{q+1-\epsilon/2}}\right)
and the third claim follows from
\alpha-(q+1)<0
by choosing
\epsilon
small enough. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2
.

By using a similar contour to that depicted in
Fig.

S5
, one can combine the definition
Eq.

S2.9
and
Lemmas

S1
and
S1.21
, in order to establish the following upper bounds on
|h_{n}(z)|
:

|h_{n}(z)|\leq\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\big(\log{n}\big),&p>1;\\
\mathcal{O}\big((\log{n})^{1+\epsilon}\big),&p=1,\end{cases}

(S5.24)

where
\epsilon>0
can be made arbitrarily small. These bounds are uniformly valid for
z=x+iy
with
|x|<1-\delta
for arbitrary but fixed
\delta>0
, and
z
in a suitable
\Theta(|x|)
-sized neighborhood of
x
.

S5.2
Uniform lower bounds for the equilibrium measure on the real line

Let us now prove
Lemma

S6
from
Section

S2.4
, which we restate here.
See
S6

Proof.

Since
h_{n}(x)
is even, it suffices to consider
x\geq 0
. The complexity of the proof depends on the magnitude of
x
, and whether it depends on
n
.
Let
M>0
be given. It is our goal to construct a corresponding constant
C>0
uniformly for sufficiently large values of
n
as stated in the lemma.

Case 1 (near origin):
x\in[0,(\log{n})^{\alpha}/\beta_{n}]
, with
\alpha>0
for
p>1
and
\alpha<1+q
for
p=1

In this near-zero regime, we can apply
Eq.

S5.16
to conclude that
h_{n}(x)=h_{n}(0)[1+o(1)]
uniformly in
x
, where the
o(1)
refers to scaling with
n
. Then the asymptotics of
h_{n}(0)
from
Eq.

S5.2
suffice to give a uniform positive lower bound on
h_{n}(x)
in this region.
Thus, any choice, say
0<C\leq 1
would work for this region.

Case 2 (bulk):
x\in[(\log{n})^{\alpha}/\beta_{n},M]
, with
\alpha
as in case 1

By
Lemma

S1
(i), there exists
A>0
such that
uQ^{\prime}(u)
is increasing for
u>A
. This implies
sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)
is increasing for
s>A/\beta_{n}
, and therefore in this region of increase
xV_{n}^{\prime}(x)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)
has the same sign as
x-s
. If we split the integral
Eq.

S2.13
for
h_{n}(x)
into two regions,

\displaystyle h_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\left(\int_{0}^{A/\beta_{n}}+\int_{A/\beta_{n}}^{1}\right)\dfrac{xV_{n}^{\prime}(x)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{x^{2}-s^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}},

(S5.25)

\displaystyle\equiv I_{1}+I_{2},

the second integral
I_{2}
is an integral of a manifestly positive integrand, so is positive. Let us first show that
I_{2}
can be lower bounded by a positive constant. Since
I_{2}
has a positive integrand, we can lower bound it by truncating the region of integration. In addition, we learn from
Lemma

S1
(v)
that for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
s\in[1/4,1]
we have

\left|sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)-\dfrac{p}{\lambda_{p}}s^{p}\right|\leq\dfrac{\epsilon}{2}.

(S5.26)

Using in addition the above mentioned monotonicity of
sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)
we obtain for
(\log{n})^{\alpha}/\beta_{n}\leq x\leq 1/2
that

\dfrac{\pi}{2}I_{2}\geq\int_{3/4}^{1}\dfrac{sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)-xV_{n}^{\prime}(x)}{s^{2}-x^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\geq\int_{3/4}^{1}\left[\dfrac{3}{4}V_{n}^{\prime}(3/4)-\dfrac{1}{2}V_{n}^{\prime}(1/2)\right]\mathrm{d}s\geq\dfrac{1}{4}\left[\dfrac{p}{\lambda_{p}}\left(\left(\dfrac{3}{4}\right)^{p}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)^{p}\right)-\epsilon\right],

(S5.27)

and for
x\in[1/2,M]
that

\dfrac{\pi}{2}I_{2}\geq\int_{1/8}^{1/4}\dfrac{xV_{n}^{\prime}(x)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{x^{2}-s^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\geq\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}\int_{1/8}^{1/4}\left[\dfrac{1}{2}V_{n}^{\prime}(1/2)-\dfrac{1}{4}V_{n}^{\prime}(1/4)\right]\mathrm{d}s\geq\dfrac{1}{8x^{2}}\left[\dfrac{p}{\lambda_{p}}\left(\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)^{p}-\left(\dfrac{1}{4}\right)^{p}\right)-\epsilon\right].

(S5.28)

From these estimates it follows that for any
p\geq 1
one can choose
\epsilon>0
and an
M
-dependent constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have
I_{2}\geq 2C
. In order to complete the proof we need to show that

|I_{1}|\leq\dfrac{I_{2}}{2},

(S5.29)

for all sufficiently large values of
n
. Let us start upper-bounding the modulus of
I_{1}
by observing

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\dfrac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{A/\beta_{n}}\dfrac{xV_{n}^{\prime}(x)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{x^{2}-s^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}},

(S5.30)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{A}\dfrac{vQ^{\prime}(v)-uQ^{\prime}(u)}{v^{2}-u^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}u}{\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}},

where
v\equiv\beta_{n}x\in[(\log{n})^{\alpha},\beta_{n}M]
, and we rescaled the integration variable for convenience. With
f_{Q}(y)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}[yQ^{\prime}(y)]=Q^{\prime}(y)+yQ^{\prime\prime}(y)
, we have

\dfrac{vQ^{\prime}(v)-uQ^{\prime}(u)}{v^{2}-u^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{v+u}\int_{0}^{1}f_{Q}[v-t(v-u)]\mathrm{d}t.

(S5.31)

The argument of
f_{Q}
is always between
u
and
v
, and since
u\in[0,A]
while
v\to\infty
, this argument is always in
[0,v]
. For bounded arguments, we know
f_{Q}(u)
is bounded due to our analyticity assumption on
Q
. For growing arguments, note that the condition
Eq.

S1.2
on
Q
implies that

\displaystyle f_{Q}(y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}(y)\left[1+\dfrac{yQ^{\prime\prime}(y)}{Q^{\prime}(y)}\right],

(S5.32)

\displaystyle\leq Q^{\prime}(y)\times 2p\mathclap{\hskip 100.00015pt\text{for large enough }y,}

(S5.33)

with
Q^{\prime}(y)>0
also for large enough
y
, given condition
Eq.

S1.1
. Again for sufficiently large
y
, from
Eq.

S1.9
we have the bound
Q^{\prime}(y)\leq y^{p-1}(\log{y})^{q+\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
. For
p>1
this bound increases with
y
, while for
p=1
it can increase or decrease depending on the sign of
q
. Taken together, these considerations of both bounded and growing arguments imply a bound, valid for
t\in[0,1]
and
u\in[0,A]
, of the form

\Big|f_{Q}[v-t(v-u)]\Big|\leq c\,v^{p-1}(\log{v})^{\eta_{q}}

(S5.34)

for some constant
c>0
, where we have defined

\eta_{q}\coloneqq\begin{cases}q+\epsilon,&q\geq 0;\\
0,&q<0,\end{cases}

(S5.35)

where
\epsilon>0
can be made small by taking
n
large. Then we have the bound

\displaystyle|I_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}c\,v^{p-1}(\log{v})^{\eta_{q}}\times\int_{0}^{A}\dfrac{1}{v+u}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}u}{\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}}.

(S5.36)

The integral can be upper bounded simply as

\int_{0}^{A}\dfrac{1}{v+u}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}u}{\sqrt{1-(u/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\leq\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-(A/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\int_{0}^{A}\dfrac{1}{v+u}\mathrm{d}u=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-(A/\beta_{n})^{2}}}\log\left(1+\dfrac{A}{v}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{v}\right),

(S5.37)

since
A/v\to 0
and
A/\beta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus we conclude that

|I_{1}|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}v^{p-2}(\log{v})^{\eta_{q}}\right).

(S5.38)

We now analyze this on a case by case basis.

Case
\mathcal{A}
:
p>1

Using
v=\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n})
and the asymptotics for
\beta_{n}
in
Eq.

S1.13
, for
p\geq 2
we have
v^{p-2}=\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}^{p-2})
, and so

|I_{1}|\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}^{p-1}}{n}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/p}\right),

(S5.39)

where the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
notation hides polylogarithmic factors which are unimportant for
p>1
. Equally, for
1<p<2
,
v^{p-2}
is
o(1)
, and hence in this case

|I_{1}|\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\beta_{n}/n)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-(p-1)/p}).

(S5.40)

Overall we conclude for all
p>1
that
|I_{1}|
vanishes as
n\to\infty
and condition (
S5.29
) is satisfied for
n
large enough.

Case
\mathcal{B}
:
p=1

In this marginal case, we need to refine our lower bound on
I_{2}
. First we rewrite the integrand of
I_{2}
in terms of
f_{Q}
as in
Eq.

S5.31
, but this time we use the bound

\displaystyle f_{Q}(y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}(y)\left[1+\dfrac{yQ^{\prime\prime}(y)}{Q^{\prime}(y)}\right],

(S5.41)

\displaystyle\geq Q^{\prime}(y)\times(p/2)\mathclap{\hskip 100.00015pt\text{for large enough }y,}

(S5.42)

which again follows from the condition
Eq.

S1.2
on
Q
. Let
A^{\prime}\geq A
be so large that we can apply
Eq.

S5.42
and the lower bound in
Eq.

S1.9
on
Q^{\prime}
throughout the slightly reduced domain of integration
[A^{\prime},\beta_{n}]
. Then, as
p=1
, we have the lower bound

I_{2}\geq\dfrac{1}{\pi}\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{A^{\prime}}^{\beta_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{\left(\log\left[v-t(v-u)\right]\right)^{q-\epsilon}}{v+u}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}u.

(S5.43)

We can take
A^{\prime}
large enough that the integrand is positive, such that we can lower bound
I_{2}
by restricting the
t
integral domain. The argument
v-t(v-u)
of the logarithm linearly interpolates between
u
and
v
, and if
q-\epsilon
is positive (negative), we restrict to the half of the
t
-integral where
v-t(v-u)
is lower (upper) bounded by its value
(v+u)/2
at the midpoint. Evaluating the integrals then gives the lower bound

I_{2}\geq\Omega\left(\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\left[\big(\log(v+u)\big)^{1+q-\epsilon}\right]_{u=A^{\prime}}^{u=\beta_{n}}\right).

(S5.44)

If
v\leq\mathcal{O}(\beta_{n}^{\delta})
for some
\delta<1
, then this bound is of the order
\Omega((\beta_{n}/n)(\log{\beta_{n}})^{1+q-\epsilon})
since the upper and lower limits do not cancel to leading order. In that case, using
Eq.

S5.38
to bound
|I_{1}|
, we have

\dfrac{|I_{1}|}{I_{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{v}\dfrac{1}{(\log{\beta_{n}})^{1+q-\epsilon-\eta_{q}}}\right).

(S5.45)

In the region
v\in[(\log{n})^{\alpha},\beta_{n}^{2/3}]
, we can bound
1/v
by
1/(\log{n})^{\alpha}
, so then

\dfrac{|I_{1}|}{I_{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{(\log{\beta_{n}})^{1+q-\epsilon-\eta_{q}+\alpha}}\right).

(S5.46)

For
q\geq 0
, the exponent is bounded by
1+\alpha-2\epsilon
, while for
q<0
, it is bounded by
1+q+\alpha-\epsilon
, which shows that in both cases the ratio goes to zero as
n\to\infty
. Equally, in the region
v\in[\beta_{n}^{2/3},M\beta_{n}]
, the
I_{2}
lower bound from
Eq.

S5.44
becomes
\Omega((\beta_{n}/n)(\log{\beta_{n}})^{q-\epsilon})
because the leading term cancels, while the
1/v
from the
I_{1}
bound is at most
1/\beta_{n}^{2/3}
, so then

\dfrac{|I_{1}|}{I_{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}^{2/3}}\dfrac{1}{(\log{\beta_{n}})^{q-\epsilon-\eta_{q}}}\right),

(S5.47)

which also goes to zero as
n\to\infty
. Thus
Eq.

S5.29
holds in all cases considered.

∎

Remark 3
.

Combining
Eqs.

S5.16
and
S5.44
, it follows that for
p=1
and
|x|<\beta_{n}^{-1/3}
, we have the uniform lower bound

h_{n}(x)\geq\Omega\big((\log{n})^{1-\epsilon}\big),

(S5.48)

where
\epsilon>0
can be made arbitrarily small by taking
n
large. (This bound remains true for
|x|<\beta_{n}^{-\delta}
with
\delta>0
.)

S5.3
Behavior of the equilibrium measure near the endpoints

S5.3.1
Behavior at the endpoint

Lemma S10
.

For
Q\in\mathrm{VSLF}(p,q)
with
p>0
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}(1)=2p.

(S5.49)

Proof.

From the integral form
Eq.

S2.13
we have

h_{n}(1)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(1)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{1-s^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}.

(S5.50)

Similar to the proof of
Eq.

S5.2
for
p>1
, we will use the result from
Lemma

S1
(v) that
\lim_{n\to\infty}sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)=(p/\lambda_{p})s^{p}
, uniformly for
s
in compact subsets of
(0,\infty)
, which proves pointwise convergence of the integrand for
s\in(0,1)
. In order to apply the dominated convergence theorem we argue differently for the integration domains
[0,1/2]
and
[1/2,1]
. Choosing
A>0
according to
Lemma

S1
(i) so that
sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)
is increasing on
[A/\beta_{n},1/2]
, the integrand is upper bounded by
(4/3)^{3/2}V_{n}^{\prime}(1)\leq\mathcal{O}(1)
on this interval. Since

\int_{0}^{A/\beta_{n}}sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\dfrac{1}{n\beta_{n}}\int_{0}^{A}uQ^{\prime}(u)\mathrm{d}u\to 0,

(S5.51)

as
n\to\infty
, we may interchange the
n
-limit and integration on the domain
[0,1/2]
. For the remaining part of the integral it suffices to show that

\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(1)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{1-s}\leq\mathcal{O}(1),

(S5.52)

uniformly for
s\in[1/2,1]
. Here we use the function
f_{Q}
introduced in the proof of
Lemma

S6
and the relations presented in
Eqs.

S5.31
and
S5.33
. We obtain

\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(1)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{1-s}=\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\dfrac{\beta_{n}Q^{\prime}(\beta_{n})-\beta_{n}sQ^{\prime}(\beta_{n}s)}{\beta_{n}-\beta_{n}s}\leq 2p\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{1}Q^{\prime}(\beta_{n}[1-t(1-s)])\mathrm{d}s.

(S5.53)

Applying
Eq.

S1.32
with
\epsilon=1
and keeping in mind that
1-t(1-s)\in[s,1]\subset[1/2,1]
for
0\leq t\leq 1
we conclude for sufficiently large
n
that

\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(1)-sV_{n}^{\prime}(s)}{1-s}\leq 2p\dfrac{2p}{\lambda_{p}}\int_{0}^{1}[1-t(1-s)]^{p-2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\mathcal{O}(1).

(S5.54)

In summary we may conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{n}(1)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\dfrac{p}{\lambda_{p}}\int_{0}^{1}\dfrac{1-s^{p}}{1-s^{2}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}=2p,

(S5.55)

where the last step follows from evaluating the integral and using the definition of
\lambda_{p}
in
Eq.

S1.11
.
∎

S5.3.2
Uniform lower bounds near
z=\pm 1

Let us now prove a uniform lower bound on
h_{n}(z)
for
z
in a neighbourhood of
z=1
, with the case
z=-1
following by symmetry. This will follow by combining the following lemma about uniform continuity with
Eq.

S5.49
, which shows that
h_{n}(1)=2p+o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma S11
.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{CVSLF}(p,q,\theta,\gamma)
and
p>0
. Then there exist constants
C,\varrho>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all sufficiently large
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

\left|h_{n}(z)-h_{n}(1)\right|\leq C|z-1|,\quad\text{for all }|z-1|\leq\varrho.

(S5.56)

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
such that the circle
\{z:|z-1|=\delta\}
is contained in the cone
C_{\theta}
. Furthermore choose
0<\varrho<\widetilde{\varrho}<\delta
. Note that the claim follows if we can establish
|h_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq C
for all
|z-1|\leq\varrho
. Since
h_{n}
is analytic in
C_{\theta}
, we may obtain such a bound from the Cauchy integral formula for derivatives

h_{n}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|s-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}}\frac{h_{n}(s)}{(s-z)^{2}}\mathrm{d}s,

(S5.57)

and from an upper bound
C_{1}
for
|h_{n}(z)|
on
\{z:|z-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}\}
that then allows us to choose
C=\widetilde{\varrho}C_{1}/(\widetilde{\varrho}-\varrho)^{2}
. In order to obtain such a bound
C_{1}
we use the representation of
Eq.

S2.11
for
h_{n}(z)
and
|z-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}
. We can deform the contour of integration to obtain

h_{n}(z)=\dfrac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1-\delta}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)-V_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{s-z}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}+\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|s-1|=\delta}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)-V_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{s-z}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{r(s)}.

(S5.58)

We have the following bounds on the integrals. First, for
|s-1|=\delta
and
|z-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}
we obtain from
Eq.

S1.29
for
n
sufficiently large that

\left|\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)-V_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{s-z}\frac{1}{r(s)}\right|\leq\dfrac{|V_{n}^{\prime}(s)|+|V_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{\left(\delta-\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\sqrt{\delta}}\leq\dfrac{4p}{\lambda_{p}}\dfrac{(1+\delta)^{p-1}}{\left(\delta-\widetilde{\varrho}\right)\sqrt{\delta}}.

(S5.59)

Second, we turn to the first summand on the right hand side of
Eq.

S5.58
. For
|z-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}
we obtain

\left|\int_{-1}^{1-\delta}\dfrac{V_{n}^{\prime}(s)-V_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{s-z}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}}\right|\leq\dfrac{1}{(\delta-\widetilde{\varrho})\sqrt{\delta}}\left[2\int_{0}^{1}|V_{n}^{\prime}(s)|\mathrm{d}s+\dfrac{4p}{\lambda_{p}}(1+\widetilde{\varrho})^{p-1}\right],

(S5.60)

for large
n
. We use
Eq.

S1.32
with
0<\epsilon<p
to bound the integral in the previous upper bound and obtain

\int_{0}^{1}|V_{n}^{\prime}(s)|\mathrm{d}s\leq\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\int_{0}^{A/\beta_{n}}|Q^{\prime}(\beta_{n}s)|\mathrm{d}s+\int_{A/\beta_{n}}^{1}s^{p-1-\epsilon}\mathrm{d}s\leq\dfrac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{A}|Q^{\prime}(u)|\mathrm{d}u+\dfrac{1}{p-\epsilon},

(S5.61)

that finally yields the desired uniform bound
C_{1}
on
|h_{n}(z)|
for
|z-1|=\widetilde{\varrho}
and large enough values of
n
.
∎

S5.4
Decay of contributions from the lens boundaries

\Delta_{n}(x)
x
\dfrac{\gamma}{\beta_{n}}
\dfrac{(\log n)^{\alpha}}{\beta_{n}}
\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}^{2/3}}
\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}^{1/3}}
1-\delta_{2}
A
B
C
D
\frac{x}{(\log n)^{\alpha}}
\frac{x}{(\log n)^{1+\alpha}}
Linear
interpolation

Figure S6:
Height function
\Delta_{n}(x)
setting the imaginary part of the lens boundary
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1}
(see
Fig.

S4
), as a function of
x=\operatorname{Re}{z}
in the different regions
A,B,C,D
. The contour is piecewise linear. The exponent
\alpha
is chosen as in
Eq.

S5.63
, and
\delta_{2}
and
\gamma
are as in
Propositions

S2
and
S3
. The specific exponents of
\beta_{n}^{2/3}
and
\beta_{n}^{1/3}
are not important; any decreasing pair of powers in
(0,1)
would suffice.

Lemma S12
.

Consider
Q\in\mathrm{CVSLF}(p,q,\theta,\gamma)
, and set
\alpha>0
arbitrarily for
p>1
but constrained to
\alpha<1+q
for
p=1
. Let the regions
A,B,C,D
and the height function
\Delta_{n}(x)
be defined as indicated in
Fig.

S6
. Then, for
\mathbb{X}\in\{A,B,C,D\}
, the following properties hold.

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\left|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(x+i\Delta_{n}(x))}\right|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

(S5.62)

n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})\int_{\mathbb{X}}\left|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(x+i\Delta_{n}(x))}\right|\mathrm{d}x\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

(S5.63)

Proof.

The proof relies on the following basic equality which follows from
Eqs.

S2.37
and
S2.41
:

\displaystyle\left|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(x+i\Delta_{n}(x))}\right|

\displaystyle=\exp\left[n\operatorname{Re}\left(\int_{0}^{\Delta_{n}(x)}ir(x+it)h_{n}(x+it)\mathrm{d}t\right)\right],

(S5.64)

where
r(z)=(z-1)^{1/2}(z+1)^{1/2}
. For
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,\Delta_{n}(x)]
, one can check that
\arg{r(x+it)}\in[\pi/4,3\pi/4]
.

We will define a function
\gamma_{n}(x)
such that we can prove the bounds

\displaystyle|h_{n}(x+it)|

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{n}(x),

(S5.65)

\displaystyle|\arg{h_{n}(x+it)}|

\displaystyle\leq\pi/6,

(S5.66)

for
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,\Delta_{n}(x)]
. Then from
Eq.

S5.64
we get

\left|e^{-2n\phi_{n}(x+i\Delta_{n}(x))}\right|\leq\exp\left[-\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{12}\right)n\gamma_{n}(x)\Delta_{n}(x)\right].

(S5.67)

We begin with the case
p=1
, and will indicate how
p>1
can be treated at the end of the proof.

Region
A

From
Eq.

S5.16
, in this region we can choose
\gamma_{n}(x)=h_{n}(0)/2
to satisfy
Eq.

S5.65
. With this choice,
Eq.

S5.66
follows from
|h_{n}(x+it)-h_{n}(0)|\leq h_{n}(0)/2
. From
Fig.

S6
we recall that
\Delta_{n}(x)=x/(\log{n})^{\alpha}
.
Eq.

S5.62
then follows from
Eq.

S5.2
and

n\Delta_{n}(x)\gamma_{n}(x)\geq\Omega\left(\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{1}{(\log{n})^{\alpha}}(\log{n})^{1-\epsilon}\right)\geq\Omega\left((\log{n})^{1+q-\alpha-2\epsilon}\right),

(S5.68)

where the second inequality uses
Eq.

S1.13
; recall that
\alpha<1+q
for
p=1
, so the exponent can always be made positive by choosing
\epsilon
small enough.
Eq.

S5.63
then follows from a simple
L_{\infty}
bound.

Regions
B
–
D

In all these regions, we choose
\gamma_{n}(x)=h_{n}(x)/2
, so that
Eqs.

S5.65
and
S5.66
follow from
|h_{n}(x+it)-h_{n}(x)|\leq h_{n}(x)/2
. Let us now verify that this is a valid choice for
\gamma_{n}(x)
. From
Remark

2
we have a uniform upper bound on
|h_{n}(z)|
in a suitable
\Theta(|x|)
-sized neighborhood of
x
. One can then use the Cauchy integral formula for derivatives to upper bound
|h_{n}^{\prime}(x+it)|\leq\mathcal{O}((\log{n})^{1+\epsilon}/|x|)
. Then we can bound the difference as
|h_{n}(x+it)-h_{n}(x)|\leq\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}(x)(\log{n})^{1+\epsilon}/|x|)
. To show that this is less than
h_{n}(x)/2
, in regions
B
&
C
we use the lower bound
h_{n}(x)\geq\Omega((\log{n})^{1-\epsilon})
from
Remark

3
, while in region
D
we use
h_{n}(x)\geq\Omega(1)
from
Lemma

S6
.

With this choice of
\gamma_{n}(x)
, one can show that in regions
C
&
D
,
Eqs.

S5.62
and
S5.63
are satisfied because
n\gamma_{n}(x)\Delta_{n}(x)\geq\Omega(n^{1/3-\epsilon})
. In region
B
,
Eq.

S5.62
follows from

n\Delta_{n}(x)\gamma_{n}(x)\geq\Omega\left(\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}(\log{n})^{1-\epsilon}\right)\geq\Omega\left((\log{n})^{1+q-2\epsilon}\right),

(S5.69)

where we used
x\geq(\log{n})^{\alpha}/\beta_{n}
, the lower bound from
Remark

3
, and
Eq.

S1.13
. Since
1+q>0
, the exponent can be made positive by choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
Eq.

S5.63
follows from the calculation

\displaystyle n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})\int_{B}\exp\left[-\Omega\left(n\,(\log{n})^{1-\epsilon}\dfrac{x}{(\log{n})^{\alpha}}\right)\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\dfrac{n\,\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})}{n(\log{n})^{1-\epsilon-\alpha}}\exp\left[-\Omega\left(\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}(\log{n})^{1-\epsilon}\right)\right]\right),

(S5.70)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\Big(\mathrm{poly}(\log{n})\exp\left[-\Omega\left((\log{n})^{1+q-2\epsilon}\right)\right]\Big),

(S5.71)

where we used
Eq.

S1.13
for
\beta_{n}
. For
q>0
the upper bound converges to zero superpolynomially in
n
, while for
q\leq 0
the convergence may be subpolynomial (but still super-polylogarithmic). This concludes the proof for
p=1
.

For
p>1
the proof is much simpler. It suffices to take
\Delta_{n}(x)=x/(\log{n})^{2}
and
\gamma_{n}(x)=(1/2)\min_{x\in[0,1]}h_{n}(x)\geq\Omega(1)
. The bound
|h_{n}(x+it)-h_{n}(x)|\leq h_{n}(x)/2
follows from
Eq.

S5.16
for
x\in A
, and for
x\in B,C,D
by an upper bound on the derivative
h_{n}^{\prime}(x+it)
using
Remark

2
as before. Overall one finds that

n\gamma_{n}(x)\Delta_{n}(x)\geq\Omega\left(\dfrac{n}{\beta_{n}}(\log{n})^{-2}\right)\geq\Omega\left(n^{1-1/p-\epsilon}\right),

(S5.72)

which suffices to prove
Eqs.

S5.62
and
S5.63
for
p>1
.
∎

S6
Airy bootstrap

Since it will be useful for
Section

X
, we include here a version of the spectral bootstrap suited for recovering the spectral function near the ‘edge’
\omega\approx\beta_{n}
. A modification is necessary because the polynomial asymptotics in this region are qualitatively different from those in the bulk of the spectrum: they are governed by the ‘Airy’ universality class rather than the ‘sine’ universality class. These Airy asymptotics hold in the ‘regular’ case where the density
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
vanishes like a square root as
\omega\to\beta_{n}
from below. For our class of spectral functions, we prove this is the case for large enough
n
(see
Section

S2.4
).

The Airy asymptotics are most easily stated in terms of a function
f_{n}(x)
which is related to
I_{n}
by

f_{n}(x)=-\left(\dfrac{3\pi}{2}\left[\dfrac{n}{2}-I_{n}(\beta_{n}x)\right]\right)^{2/3}.

(S6.1)

Since
I_{n}(\beta_{n})=\int_{0}^{\beta_{n}}\sigma_{n}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega=n/2
, we have
f_{n}(1)=0
. Moreover, given the square root vanishing of the derivative
\partial_{\omega}I_{n}(\omega)=\sigma_{n}(\omega)
, using
Eqs.

51
and
55
we have

f_{n}^{\prime}(1)=\left(\dfrac{nh_{n}(1)}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2/3}.

(S6.2)

The asymptotics of
h_{n}(1)
are given in
Eq.

57
, where it is shown that
h_{n}(1)
is
\mathcal{O}(1)
as
n\to\infty
, so
f_{n}^{\prime}(1)\sim\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})
.

For the Airy bootstrap, we start at the endpoint
\omega=\beta_{n}
, and then work backwards to lower frequencies. The derivation of the following equations is given in
Section

S4.2.3
.

First we approximate
h_{n}(1)
using

h_{n}(1)\approx\dfrac{1}{2n}\left(\dfrac{\Ai(0)}{\Ai^{\prime}(0)}\right)^{3}\left[\rho-\left(\dfrac{p_{n}(\beta_{n})+p_{n-1}(\beta_{n})}{p_{n}(\beta_{n})-p_{n-1}(\beta_{n})}\right)\right]^{3},

(S6.3)

where
\Ai
is the Airy function. Then we can estimate the spectral function at
\omega=\beta_{n}
using
Eq.

S6.2
and

\dfrac{\Phi(\beta_{n})}{2\pi}\approx\dfrac{1}{\beta_{n}p_{n}(\beta_{n})^{2}}\left[\big(2f_{n}^{\prime}(1)\big)^{1/4}\Ai(0)-(1+\rho)\big(2f_{n}^{\prime}(1)\big)^{-1/4}\Ai^{\prime}(0)\right].

(S6.4)

We can now proceed with the main bootstrap equations: with
x\equiv\omega/\beta_{n}
, the update of the spectral function is given by

\displaystyle\dfrac{\Phi(\omega)}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{2}{\beta_{n}}\dfrac{1}{p_{n}(\omega)^{2}+p_{n-1}(\omega)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\Big[-2x\Ai\big(f_{n}(x)\big)\Ai^{\prime}\big(f_{n}(x)\big)\sin\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]

(S6.5)

\displaystyle+\big(-f_{n}(x)\big)^{1/2}\Ai\big(f_{n}(x)\big)^{2}\big(x\cos\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]+1\big)-\big(-f_{n}(x)\big)^{-1/2}\Ai^{\prime}\big(f_{n}(x)\big)^{2}\big(x\cos\left[\rho\arccos{x}\right]-1\big)\Big],

while the update of the phase function
f_{n}(x)
is given by

\displaystyle f_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\approx\dfrac{1}{2\Ai^{2}f_{n}^{2}-2(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}f_{n}-\Ai\Ai^{\prime}}\Big[-2\beta_{n}\dfrac{\Phi(\omega)}{2\pi}K_{n}(\omega,\omega)f_{n}+\frac{2}{1-x^{2}}\Ai\Ai^{\prime}\cos[\rho\arccos{x}]f_{n}

(S6.6)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\sin[\rho\arccos{x}]}{1-x^{2}}\left(\Ai^{2}\big(-f_{n}\big)^{3/2}-(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}(-f_{n})^{1/2}\right)-\dfrac{\rho}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\left(\Ai^{2}\big(-f_{n}\big)^{3/2}+(\Ai^{\prime})^{2}(-f_{n})^{1/2}\right)\Big],

where we have suppressed the arguments of
f_{n}\equiv f_{n}(x)
,
\Ai\equiv\Ai(f_{n}(x))
, and
\Ai^{\prime}\equiv\Ai^{\prime}(f_{n}(x))
.

Given a choice of frequency spacing
0<\delta\omega\ll 1
and a minimum frequency
\omega_{\mathrm{min}}
, the main routine then works as follows.

Figure S7:
(a,b)
Log- and linear-scale spectral function
\Phi(\omega)
of the energy current operator of the mixed field Ising model, as computed using the ‘bulk’ spectral bootstrap of
Section

IX.1
and the ‘Airy’ spectral bootstrap of
Section

S6
. While they are intended to work in different frequency regimes, the estimates of
\Phi(\omega)
agree remarkably well throughout the bulk.
(c)
Comparison between the extracted equilibrium measure
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
for the same model. They agree throughout the bulk, but the bulk bootstrap breaks down near
\omega=\beta_{n}
, whereas the Airy bootstrap produces the expected square root vanishing of
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
as
\omega\to\beta_{n}
. Indeed, the inset shows
h_{n}(x\equiv\omega/\beta_{n})=2\pi\frac{\beta_{n}}{n}\frac{\sigma_{n}(\omega)}{\sqrt{1-(\omega/\beta_{n})^{2}}}
; for the Airy bootstrap this is finite and as
x\to 1
approaches the value
h_{n}(1)\approx 2
expected from
Eq.

57
with
p=1
. Here we set
n=40
, with
\beta_{n}\approx 2b_{n}\approx 46.1
.

0.

Set
\omega=\beta_{n}
and
f_{n}(1)=0
. Compute
f_{n}^{\prime}(1)
using
Eqs.

S6.2
and
S6.3
, and
\Phi(\beta_{n})
using
Eq.

S6.4
.

1.

Increment
\omega\mapsto\omega-\delta\omega
.

2.

Set
f_{n}\left(\dfrac{\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)=f_{n}\left(\dfrac{\omega+\delta\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)-f_{n}^{\prime}\left(\dfrac{\omega+\delta\omega}{\beta_{n}}\right)\times\dfrac{\delta\omega}{\beta_{n}}
.

3.

Compute
\Phi(\omega)
using
Eq.

S6.5
.

4.

Compute
f_{n}^{\prime}(\omega/\beta_{n})
using
Eq.

S6.6
.

5.

Repeat steps 1–4 until
\omega=\omega_{\mathrm{min}}
, then terminate.

Note that in step 2 we divide the derivative by
\beta_{n}
because
f_{n}(x)
is defined in terms of
x\equiv\omega/\beta_{n}
.

In
Fig.

S7
we show a benchmark of this ‘Airy bootstrap’ on the energy current operator of the mixed field Ising model (MFIM), as we did in
Section

IX.1
, and compare to the results from the ‘bulk bootstrap’ described in that section. These two methods are designed to work in different frequency regimes: the bulk bootstrap starts at
\omega=0
and iterates up to some
\omega_{\mathrm{max}}\ll\beta_{n}
, while the Airy bootstrap starts at
\omega=\beta_{n}
and iterates down to some
\omega_{\mathrm{min}}
. They should agree in frequency regimes where both methods are approximately valid, but
a priori
they could disagree elsewhere. In fact, in
Fig.

S7
(a,b), we find that the extracted spectral functions
\Phi(\omega)
agree remarkably well throughout almost the whole frequency range
\omega\in[0,\beta_{n}]
, with only some small deviation at
\omega\approx\beta_{n}
. This good agreement lends credence to our investigation of the high-frequency tail of
\Phi(\omega)
in
Section

IX.1
, where we found
\Phi(\omega\to\infty)\sim\exp[-\mathcal{O}(\omega\log{\omega})]
, consistent with locality bounds on operator growth in 1D
[
11
]
. It is also perhaps surprising that the Airy bootstrap can produce accurate estimates of the spectral function at moderate frequencies, since one might initially have worried about the numerical stability of a procedure which begins with the spectral function as small as
\Phi(\omega=\beta_{n})\sim 10^{-43}
.

More significant deviation between the bulk and Airy bootstraps can be found by looking at their estimates for the equilibrium measure
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
, as shown in
Fig.

S7
(c). This is computed directly as part of the bulk bootstrap, while for the Airy bootstrap we can recover it from
f_{n}(\omega/\beta_{n})
using
Eq.

S6.1
and
\sigma_{n}(\omega)=\partial_{\omega}I_{n}(\omega)
. We find that the two methods give closely matching estimates of
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
for
0\leq\omega/\beta_{n}\lesssim 0.9
, but as
\omega
approaches the spectral edge at
\omega=\beta_{n}
, the bulk bootstrap estimate blows up, while the Airy bootstrap produces the square root vanishing expected for ‘regular’ equilibrium measures (we remind the reader that we prove that this regularity property holds at large-
n
for our class of spectral function—see
Section

S2.4
). To corroborate this, in the inset to
Fig.

S7
(c) we plot the function (c.f.
Eqs.

51
and
55
)

h_{n}(x\equiv\omega/\beta_{n})=2\pi\dfrac{\beta_{n}}{n}\dfrac{\sigma_{n}(\omega)}{\sqrt{1-(\omega/\beta_{n})^{2}}},

(S6.7)

which should be finite at
x=1
if
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
vanishes like a square root at
\omega=\beta_{n}
. For the Airy bootstrap estimate of
h_{n}(x)
, this is indeed the case, and we can also see that the limiting value of
h_{n}(x)
as
x\to 1
is close to 2, which is expected from
Eq.

57
with
p=1
, given the
\Phi(\omega\to\infty)\sim\exp[-\mathcal{O}(\omega\log{\omega})]
high frequency decay of the spectral function. On the other hand, the bulk bootstrap estimate does not successfully capture this behavior. Although the results of
Fig.

S7
(a,b) show that this disagreement in estimating
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
is not fatal for approximating the spectral function, it will be more relevant that we have an accurate estimate of
\sigma_{n}(\omega)
in
Section

X
, where we check for the emergence of universality.

Glossary of Symbols

We use standard asymptotic notation. Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be two functions. We write
f(x)=\mathcal{O}(g(x))
iff there exist constants
C,x_{0}>0
such that
|f(x)|<C|g(x)|
for all
x>x_{0}
. We write
f(x)=o(g(x))
iff, for every constant
C>0
, there exists
x_{0}>0
such that
|f(x)|<C|g(x)|
for all
x>x_{0}
. We write
f(x)=\Omega(g(x))
iff
g(x)=\mathcal{O}(f(x))
, and
f(x)=\omega(g(x))
iff
g(x)=o(f(x))
. We write
f(x)=\Theta(g(x))
iff
f(x)=\mathcal{O}(g(x))
and
f(x)=\Omega(g(x))
. We write
f(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(g(x))
iff
f(x)=\mathcal{O}(g(x)(\log|g(x)|)^{k})
for some finite
k
, and
f(x)=\widetilde{\Omega}(g(x))
iff
g(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(f(x))
. We write
f(x)=\mathrm{poly}(g(x))
iff
f(x)=\mathcal{O}((g(x))^{k})
for some finite
k
.

Symbol

Description

Definition

\mathcal{L}

Liouvillian superoperator

\mathcal{L}(\cdot)=[H,\cdot\,]

A

Initial Lanczos operator (not necessarily normalized)

System dependent

C(t)

Autocorrelation function

C(t)=(A|A(t))=(A|e^{i\mathcal{L}t}A)

Spectral function

\Phi(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\omega t}C(t)\mathrm{d}t

\rho

Low-frequency power-law exponent

\Phi(\omega\to 0)\sim|\omega|^{\rho}

G(z)

Green’s function (resolvent)

G(z)=\left(A\left|\frac{1}{z-\mathcal{L}}\right|A\right)

Level-
n

\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}(\omega)

n
th spectral function (w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}

Degree

Eq.

Monic orthogonal polynomial

Potential (for Coulomb gas)

Defined implicitly by

(see

Christoffel-Darboux kernel

Weighted Christoffel-Darboux kernel

Sine kernel

\mathbb{S}(u,v)=\frac{\sin[\pi(u-v)]}{\pi(u-v)}
